What broad questions should you have prepared ahead of a STEM interview?
2026-09-08

What broad questions should you have prepared ahead of a STEM interview?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
STEM is a broad area, but some interview topics will come up time and time again, regardless of your field, so make sure you have answers ready to go. There are far more STEM careers than are possible to list here, in areas such as pharmaceuticals, medtech, finance, science, research, quantum, cybersecurity and so on, all of which come with unique requirements, expectations and opportunities. That is to say that STEM is such a broad field you wouldn’t be blamed for assuming that, when it comes to interviewing for a position, there is very little overlap from one role or discipline to the next. But that is not the case. In fact, regardless of the chosen route within STEM, often – by developing a broad knowledge foundation and having a ‘standard’ set of answers ready to mould as needs be – you ensure that you are significantly prepared. So, what kinds of broad interview questions should you think about in order to cover a range of STEM disciplines? Whether you are looking for a new job in security, analytics, fintech, science or one of the hundreds of other STEM-based career routes, you can almost guarantee that you will be asked questions that explore who you are as a person and how you found yourself choosing this field. Expect to be asked: What inspired your interest in STEM? How do you intend to apply your early learnings to current college or career ambitions? What elements of your personality lend themselves to a STEM career? What STEM subjects did you undertake as part of your secondary or higher education? It is an opportunity for you to show that a career in STEM is about more than simply having a job, but also an ingrained love for discovery and exploration. While an employer is going to appreciate seeing how STEM was always a core feature of your early life and education, they are also going to require proof that you sought out opportunities to advance your skills and knowledge. This might take the form of questions such as: How have you engaged with upskilling? What resources do you use to advance your knowledge? What learning opportunities have you sought out via the wider STEM community, extracurriculars and competitions? This is where you can showcase any projects you might have undertaken; a portfolio is ideal for this situation. You should also discuss any microcredentials, online courses or even just research that you have followed. It is an area of questioning that is not solely about what you have achieved or learned, but rather is a way of determining how motivated you are and whether or not you can work independently. Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Think for impact with Liberty IT. Delivering global software solutions Skills, achievements and educational certificates are all forms of quantifiable proof that you can operate in the STEM space. But career longevity is about more than racking up achievements – it is about showing that you know how to make them translate to the work you do every single day. With that in mind, you should expect questions along the lines of: Can you tell me about a time when you applied problem-solving skills to a real-world issue? How do you address challenges in the workplace? How might you defuse workplace conflict? How do you handle stress? In the same way that questions around practical or hard skills are often used to determine how you engage in learning, questions around the workplace environment and soft skills are often used to determine behaviour, which is critical to a productive and harmonious workplace. Yes, they want to know if you can communicate well – but overall, it is about determining whether or not you fit with their company’s culture. This is also one area in which you can also expect questions to be far more specific to your area of expertise. However, the mode of questioning remains broad. For example, if you are in cybersecurity, you may be asked to discuss how you managed a threat. If you are in analytics or research, you might discuss your preferred methods of data collection, or how you offset bias and implement ethics. While it is true that the vast majority of any job interview, in the STEM space or not, is going to centre around the jobseeker, their goals and compatibility, companies also want to hear a little about themselves. An organisation needs to be confident that the person they are interviewing understands how the business operates, its mission and what a career with them looks like. Ensure that you don’t go into any interview unprepared to answer the following: Why do you want to work here? How did you hear about this position? What about us makes you think you would be a good fit? How do you see yourself progressing here? What are your thoughts on the work being carried out here? And perhaps most importantly of all: Do you have any questions for us? This shows that you are still interested in the position, that you are curious about what a role there could look like and that you understand basic interview etiquette. It is unlikely that any STEM interviewer would neglect to ask questions in this vein. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. New Irish dispute body to tackle illegal online content
2026-09-08

New Irish dispute body to tackle illegal online content

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Another operator, Appeals Centre Europe, began providing dispute resolution services in Ireland in 2024. UK-based independent press regulator Impress is launching a new service in Ireland to help take down illegal content, such as AI-generated deepfakes, from social media. Impress Dispute Resolutions (IDR) – Impress’s Dublin subsidiary – is an out-of-court dispute settlement (ODS) body certified by Ireland’s media regulator Coimisiún na Meán. Launching today (8 September), it is offering a free-of-charge, independent mechanism for EU citizens to contest harmful content and platform decisions under the EU’s Digital Services Act (DSA). IDR’s scheme is available to users in Ireland and the EU, and covers major platforms including Instagram, Facebook, TikTok and YouTube. The DSA mandates that online platforms must provide a clear and accessible means to lodge complaints against illegal content. They must also allow users to request a review if they disagree with a platform’s decision. In addition, the law also provides users the ability to turn to ODS bodies for content disputes, which offers a faster and more cost-effective way to settle disputes with social media platforms when compared to much slower and more costly judicial routes. These bodies are generally provide services at a low or no cost. While ODS bodies cannot impose a binding settlement, platforms and users are required to engage with them in good faith. “Over the past decade, we’ve supported thousands of people harmed in the media – and demand has never been higher,” said IDR co-founder Gia Thom. “We’re nonprofit by design, which keeps IDR independent, credible and focused on strengthening the information ecosystem.” The EU has 11 listed ODS services spread across the bloc, including the Appeals Centre Europe, which began providing ODS services in Ireland in 2024. Safety on social media has come to a head in recent years, with parents and lawmakers moving to shield children from the provable harms posed online by popular platforms. Meanwhile, the proliferation of illegal content is becoming compounded by AI, which enables bad actors to generate realistic-looking (often non-consensual and sexual) images and videos in seconds. IDR, in addition to dealing with AI deepfakes, can also help users navigate disputes around illegal or harmful speech, negative impacts on civic discourse or elections, and data protection, among other areas. “Impress has delivered independent and effective redress for the public harmed by the media for over a decade, and we are very pleased to extend our proven service to those harmed by decisions of tech platforms,” said Impress CEO Lexie Kirkconnell-Kawana. “We know intimately and rise to the challenge of balancing the rights of individuals, groups and organisations while upholding the law and protecting free speech. Impress represents integrity in all that we do, and we will continue to embody this principle in providing fair outcomes for all participants to our scheme.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. MTU and Tyndall debut course to tackle chip talent shortages

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The institutions will collaborate on a postgraduate programme designed to develop a sturdy talent pipeline for the semiconductor industry. Munster Technological University (MTU) in partnership with the Tyndall National Institute in Cork, has developed a new postgraduate programme with the aim of addressing the global shortage of technicians and engineers required to work in specialised semiconductor cleanroom facilities known as nanofabs. The programme is specifically geared towards those working in technical roles in semiconductor cleanrooms – which are controlled environments specifically engineered to protect highly sensitive semiconductor wafers – who want to upskill and earn a postgraduate qualification. It is also for recent STEM undergraduates looking to begin their career in specialisation in cleanroom-based technology and fabrication processes. The educational elements will be delivered by academic and technical staff with previous semiconductor cleanroom experience and will explore a range of topics, including the core discipline areas of semiconductor cleanroom processing, nanometrology and cleanroom utilities and maintenance. It is also well suited to students looking for more flexibility, as it is delivered online allowing learners to attend lectures remotely. They will also be expected to use their daily practical experience to contribute to assessment work. Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Life-changing career opportunities for you Always at the cutting edge of new innovation The postgraduate programme aims to contribute to the development of Ireland’s specialist talent pipeline by producing graduates with advanced knowledge and skills, bringing it in line with the goals of the national and global semiconductor strategies. The start date for the course is 14 September. In late June, MTU also launched a new master’s of engineering in integrated manufacturing engineering, as a means of meeting the growing demand for advanced engineering skills across Ireland’s manufacturing sector. The level 9, part-time programme, which will be delivered from the MTU Kerry campus, will aim to develop skills in sustainable manufacturing, industrial automation, artificial intelligence, data engineering and Industry 4.0 technologies. Meanwhile, in February of this year, Maynooth University launched a new master’s degree in circuit design that aims to deliver skilled workers in the semiconductor sector in alignment with the Irish Government’s ‘Silicon Island’ strategy. Mistral valued at €21bn after €3bn Series D funding round
2026-09-08

Mistral valued at €21bn after €3bn Series D funding round

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Mistral said the funding would be used to ‘significantly expand’ its frontier research. French AI start-up Mistral has raised €3bn in Series D funding at a post-money valuation of more than €21bn. The round, which the company said is “the largest equity fundraising round ever completed by a European technology company”, was led by Samsung Electronics with participation from the EU’s Scaleup Europe Fund and PSG Equity, among others. Mistral said the funding would be used to “significantly expand [its] frontier research” – which it described as “the foundation underpinning its infrastructure, products and sovereignty” – as well as scaling its compute capacity for training AI models, expanding its infrastructure, and growing commercially and internationally. In its funding announcement, the Paris-based company, founded in 2023, said it is focused on more than simply creating ever-more powerful AI models, and that the new central challenge for organisations and governments is in “how to harness the power of AI for their mission-critical needs without surrendering control over the infrastructure and intelligence loop”. Mistral said it is “the only AI company in the world building the full stack” of open-weight models, infrastructure, compute capacity and products. It described its stack as a “sovereign AI layer” that promises ringfenced customer data, adaptable models, private computing capacity, and controllable and auditable AI systems. The details of the new funding round are broadly in line with earlier reports that emerged in July. In March, Mistral raised $830m in its first debt financing, for the purposes of funding its data centre near Paris. At the time, the company said the deal, supported by a consortium of seven “top-tier” global banks, would pay for Nvidia Grace Blackwell infrastructure with 13,800 Nvidia GB300 GPUs at the “cutting-edge” centre, bringing powered capacity to 44MW. Mistral – which said it operates across 20 countries and supports more than 125 global enterprises, such as Airbus, ASML and HSBC, in their “mission-critical AI transformation” – secured Series C funding of €1.7bn at a post-money valuation of €11.7bn a year ago, in a round led by Dutch chipmaking giant ASML. The French AI company is seen as one of Europe’s frontrunners in the sector. However, its funding and valuations to date are much lower than the dominant US duo of Anthropic and OpenAI, which are each currently valued at nearly $1trn. Media reports over the summer also placed higher – but more comparable – valuations on Mistral’s Chinese AI competitors such as Moonshot and DeepSeek. Anthropic is backing away from talks to acquire Decart AI, a research start-up building technology that improves chip efficiency, Bloomberg reported today (8 September). The deal was expected to be worth around $6bn, significantly higher than Decart’s reported valuation of nearly $4bn following a $300m raise earlier this year. Backed by Nvidia, Benchmark, Adobe, Sequoia and Radical Ventures, among others, Decart’s AI infrastructure aims to improves training and inferencing capacities of AI models. Its foundational model Lucy supposedly edits live video at the speed of a stream, while its so-called ‘world model’ Oasis, designed for physical AI, generates realistic simulations to train robots. The acquisition was expected to help Anthropic’s compute infrastructure absorb some of its growing demand challenges. The AI giant, according to The Information, has signed some $517bn in compute capacity leases over the past 11 months, which amounts to around 14.8GW of capacity. The company also confirmed last month that it would be designing its own chips to circumvent the worldwide shortage in the technology and access a steady supply. Headquartered in California, Decart was founded in 2023 by former Israeli Defence Forces intelligence unit soldiers – brothers Dean and Orian Leitersdorf and Moshe Shalev. Its $300m May raise was led by Radical Ventures and its co-founder Jordan Jacobs, and brought Decart’s total funding to more than $450m. Reports of the failed Decart acquisition come as Anthropic is expected to delay marketing its initial public offering prospectus to mid-October. The AI giant’s highly anticipated public listing, investors hope, will mark the largest debut in history by raising the company $75bn or more at a $2trn valuation. Last month, a US judge blocked the Pentagon’s blacklisting of Anthropic’s products for government use, handing the AI company a major win ahead of its plans to go public. The company sued the US government in March after its refusal to lower guardrails on its AI models led to an abrupt ‘supply chain risk’ designation. AI School Hooking Children Up to Heart Monitor in Front of Class While Playing Critiques of Their Work
2026-09-08

AI School Hooking Children Up to Heart Monitor in Front of Class While Playing Critiques of Their Work

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Schools across the world are experimenting with AI tools, usually to the dismay of unsuspecting parents. But one privately-run school is explicitly embracing AI as part of its sales pitch, and the in-class experience sounds more bizarre than anything you’d imagine. A blistering new article by education policy writer and cognitive scientist Benjamin Riley took a look under the hood of Alpha School, a private K-12 school company in the US which boasts an AI-based teaching model, among other highly dubious features. To make the grade, high school students are required to pass an eight-week “bootcamp.” After being invited to follow social media accounts of other students at Alpha School, Riley observed a process more akin to hazing than high school orientation, as the private company made its prospective pupils jump through increasingly degrading hoops to secure their spot on the roster. For example, students in the bootcamp are required to start a brand-new account on X-formerly-Twitter, for which they’re required to get a minimum of 100 organic followers, pay for X Premium, and post an unknown number of replies to “bigger accounts with real substance,” as one student explained. “‘Great post’ replies don’t count,” the anonymous student posted in a screenshot shared by Riley. “Buying followers or spamming = automatic fail.” But forced social media assignments are just the beginning. As part of the bootcamp, students are also required to pass an eyebrow-raising heart rate challenge, in which they’re hooked up to live monitors in front of their classmates and presented with a pre-recorded video of their parents berating them. “The challenge rule was simple,” one student wrote: “my resting heart rate was 80bpm, and I had to stay within 10 percent of that the whole time. Anything above 88 was a fail.” It isn’t clear what educational purpose this particular trial was supposed to serve, or what the penalty would be for students who fail the heart rate challenge, or the boot camp overall. At least one student said the eight-week hazing period is meant to “prove I deserve to be here,” though Alpha School didn’t comment on Riley’s inquiry. “I ended up averaging 87bpm,” the student continued, evidently just barely making the grade. “Barely made the cutoff by one single beat, and my hands were shaking the entire rest of the afternoon.” As part of boot camp, Alpha School students apparently have their heart rates monitored as they watch recorded videos of their parents critiquing them. This activity takes place in front of their classmates, and students must keep their heart rates within 10 percent of their baseline to pass. Additionally, Riley noted that Alpha School “requires students to do unpaid labor cleaning restaurant kitchens or gas-station bathrooms” in a test of “grit and hard work.” This “assignment,” confirmed by two Alpha School pupils, involved students recording themselves and narrating their tasks, with any complaint or negative comment resulting in an automatic fail. “We have to find and execute an unglamorous job with zero pay, whether that means mucking horse stalls, cleaning grease traps after closing, washing trash bins, or scrubbing gas station bathrooms,” one of the students explained. Other challenges abound, like a requirement to complete an entire unit of a standard Advanced Placement course without a teacher — AI chatbots only — and pass an exam within just five days. One 14-year-old student wrote that they were required to survive for three days in an unfamiliar section of wilderness “with only 3 bags of rice and a tent.” Contacted by Riley for comment, Alpha School flipped the script, hounding the writer for daring to report on the “social media activity of minor children.” “We ask that you weigh the ethics of identifying minors in this way in your reporting, and we expect any published piece to protect their identities,” an Alpha School representative said. Responding to Riley’s story, educator and writer Audrey Watters said that “Alpha School’s promises have always been mostly snake-oil: this idea that artificial intelligence can deliver high-speed, high-quality education is mostly the stuff of science fiction, not reality.” “What we witness with Alpha School isn’t a matter of simply ignoring the weatherman, fingers-crossed hoping that the sun will continue to shine on the chosen few and that the winds will shift again in industry’s favor,” Watters continued, referring to rising outrage against AI, and the Silicon Valley-ethos underlying Alpha School’s mission. “Rather, it’s pretty clear that there’s an absence of any moral compass at all.” More on AI in education: The Results of Middle and High School Students Using AI Are Extremely Ominous

Elon Musk Seems Legitimately Shaken Over This New Documentary

It’s no secret that Elon Musk is hiding plenty of skeletons in his closet. The mercurial billionaire has made an extensive list of enemies through his offensive commentary, led the gutting of humanitarian aid, exploited workers at his companies, and meddled with the democratic process — all the while alienating investors with his chronic overpromising and underdelivering. Simply put, documentarian and investigative journalist Alex Gibney had a very rich source of material to draw from for his upcoming documentary “Musk,” which had its world premiere today at the ongoing Venice Film Festival. According to journalist David Sirota, Musk is “freaking out” about the film because Gibney “doesn’t pull punches, doesn’t cower and won’t be bullied.” “And so the bully is mad,” Sirota tweeted. By all accounts, the four-hour epic will be a doozy. The documentarian “crafted a movie that, while not new in its specifics, is often damning in its totality, standing apart from anything the media entertainment complex has recently turned out — a portrait of power with the cinematic scope of ‘All the King’s Men’ and the potential social impact of ‘The Pentagon Papers,'” the Hollywood Reporter gushed. Musk has reportedly gone to extreme — and sometimes unsuccessful — lengths to silence those involved in Gibney’s latest work. Musk’s ex and former conservative influencer Ashley St. Clair, who is “prominently featured” in the documentary, told reporters at the Venice Film Festival that she was “offered the opportunity not to speak for $40 million,” as quoted by People. “When you’re offered something like that [to stay quiet], it really causes a debate in your head about what your speech means and how valuable that is and why do they want to offer me this amount of money to not speak?” St. Clair said. St. Clair is one of four women who have had a total of 14 children with Musk, at least that we know about so far. The 26-year-old had a major falling out with the entrepreneur, going as far as to allege that the billionaire “financially retaliated” against her for filing a custody petition by reducing child support payments. We’re likely to hear more commentary from Musk about the film. Last week, Musk tweeted that Gibney was “obviously going to make the most convincingly terrible hit piece on me that he can possibly think of,” calling him a “douchebag to his core.” But considering the staggering amount of material Musk has gifted the world, Gibney had plenty of material to work with. Fashion Founder Emily Oberg Says She Tried Using AI for Photo Shoots, and It Was a Failure Because It Will “Never Possess Good Taste” Like Humans Do
2026-09-08

Fashion Founder Emily Oberg Says She Tried Using AI for Photo Shoots, and It Was a Failure Because It Will “Never Possess Good Taste” Like Humans Do

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Can AI have good taste? Fashion founder and tastemaker Emily Oberg says no. In a post on her Substack, Emily Loves, Oberg discussed her experiments using AI to help her run Sporty & Rich, the fashion and lifestyle brand she founded in 2018 on the back of a popular moodboard she created. One area where she’s been testing AI, she wrote, is by using AI to generate e-commerce images — and according to the founder’s postmortem, the test was a failure. “With the rise of AI and the ongoing discourse around the threat it has on creativity, jobs and society as a whole, I firmly believe that it will never possess good taste the way that a human does,” Oberg declared. “And this is why I don’t think it’s as much of a threat to creatives as people think.” E-commerce imagery, Oberg said, is one of her brand’s steepest expenses, costing “upwards of” $200,000 for each twice-yearly shoot. Wanting to see if AI could help her get around these significant costs, Sporty & Rich used AI to generate e-commerce images to model its last collection. While the imagery may have been far cheaper than photos modeled, photographed, and edited by humans, however, the cost-cutting came with a catch: it just wasn’t very good. “This was one area I thought we could try to save on but after having seen the result, I’m now going back to producing our [e-commerce] with a full team of humans, because AI could never achieve the results that my IRL team could,” Oberg reflected, adding that her brand’s current images have a “certain lifelessness to them.” “They’re flat, the styling is mediocre and the images are too ‘perfect’ in a bad way,” Oberg continued. “The models look dead in the eyes and don’t have any organic movement whatsoever. The images are empty and void of any feeling or emotion.” Oberg chalked up the AI’s failure to the reality that taste is “felt, not learned.” And image-generating AI models are, after all, learning models; they don’t have lived experiences that translate into a particular way of viewing the world around them and the objects within it, nor do they have a certain “je ne sais quoi.” Though taste is a way through which people communicate with each other, taste itself is something that’s often unexplainable. “The smartest AI in the world can learn everything there is to know about taste and style,” she added, “but the feeling will never be there.” It’s interesting to see this AI experiment fail at Sporty & Rich, a brand that, like peers including Glossier and Goop, is a definitional of a certain type of deeply aesthetics-driven venture pioneered on social media platforms like Pinterest, Tumblr, and Instagram during the 2010s. Oberg was able to capture a feeling, first translated through a collection of carefully-curated imagery from across the web, and bottle it into a successful line of fashion and lifestyle products designed to embody a particular “health is wealth” ethos. That today’s AI models apparently can’t adequately capture that same feeling or aesthetic in a way that feels satisfying, or even human, speaks volumes to its remaining limits in the creative world. People “who truly have good taste don’t follow trends, they set them,” wrote Oberg. “More than this, they set them without trying to, it happens by just being themselves and liking what they like, not because an algorithm or AI told them to.” A key question that remains is whether other brands will continue to view a human touch worth it for the cost — or if they’ll charge ahead with AI nonetheless. Scientists Say LLMs Appear to Be Acting as a Cognitive Virus Among Humans
2026-09-08

Scientists Say LLMs Appear to Be Acting as a Cognitive Virus Among Humans

Futurism | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Generative AI has infiltrated almost every aspect of our daily lives, whether we like it or not. It’s nearly impossible to avoid on social media, and it’s even showing up on dinner menus at the local diner. Some researchers have suggested that we’re already adapting our own behavior to suit the demands of AI tech. AI’s wide proliferation shares some striking similarities with the ways viruses spread, as an international team of researchers argue in a new paper that has yet to be peer reviewed. They set out to lay out a new theoretical framework about how we’re increasingly becoming hooked on tools like OpenAI’s ChatGPT and Anthropic’s Claude, and how it’s changing our collective ways of understanding the world around us. “Here we introduce the idea of [large language models] as cognitive viruses,” they write. “Culturally transmitted technologies whose spread is promoted by their usefulness but can also increase dependence through cognitive offloading.” Researchers have previously warned that AI is actively damaging our cognitive abilities. Students, in particular, are choosing to use AI tools in the classroom, allowing them to offload much of their critical thinking. “More generally, humans have always externalized parts of cognition into language, writing, institutions, and technology,” the researchers write. “This externalization has been the engine of rapid and accelerated change driven by culture. AI represents a new step in this process, one in which external cognitive machinery becomes increasingly active and agent-like.” This transition to adapting LLMs also “has a contagion-like component,” because the spread occurs “because people learn from other people.” It’s a slippery slope, the researchers argue, as the “loss of autonomy becomes self-reinforcing and the population can move rapidly toward a state of much stronger cognitive offloading and lower cognitive competence.” Put simply, the widespread use of LLMs could be making us much dumber than we were before — not to mention wreaking complex psychological and sociological changes that we’re only starting to understand. At the same time, there are ways to protect ourselves from being infected through a process the researchers dub “cognitive immunization.” “At the population level, immunization does not mean preventing contact with AI,” the paper reads. “It means preserving the practices and institutions that keep human cognition active: unaided problem solving, verification, critical discussion, periods of deliberate disengagement, maintenance of non-AI skills, and educational designs in which the model supports rather than completes the cognitive task.” More on cognitive offloading: Your Coworkers Are Becoming “Meat Proxies” for AI

Musk has bet the company’s entire future on the success of its Cybercabs and its humanoid robot, dubbed Optimus. With neither showing any signs of a significant public rollout any time soon, Wall Street is bound to keep asking some tough questions. TechCrunch
Hackers are stealing Claude tokens from subscribers
2026-09-08

Hackers are stealing Claude tokens from subscribers

TechCrunch | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt On August 4, Grant De Swardt, an independent AI consultant in East Sussex, U.K., noticed something strange going on with his Claude Max 20x account. He hadn’t been working that day, yet his token usage was climbing. The next day, he disabled everything he had attached to Claude and did not work with it. Token consumption again increased. “In the clearest controlled interval, it increased from 45% to 55% while I performed no work, scheduled Cowork tasks were paused or completed, Dispatch/cloud execution was disabled, and there was no corresponding active local Claude Code task,” De Swardt told TechCrunch. What was eating up his token allowance? He had no idea, so he contacted Anthropic and asked for an itemized list. Anthropic didn’t provide one, but it agreed something was off. It suspended his paid account, invalidated all of his sessions and server-side Claude Code tokens, and issued him a partial refund of £44.49 for the remaining time on his $200-per-month subscription. The suspension wreaked havok on his business, he told TechCrunch. His job is to help small and mid-size businesses set up agents — a sort of forward-deployed engineer for hire — for tasks like automatically loading purchase-order data from emails into the accounting software. As a sole proprietor, he relies on agents throughout his whole business, too: daily admin tasks, website design, coding. “Like everything is just running through AI these days,” he said. After investigating, Anthropic told De Swardt it found the culprit: A compromised Claude session key was used to mint unauthorized Claude Code OAuth tokens. The company told him the account “appeared to have been used by an unauthorized-looking third-party service to handle activity for other people, but they could not determine how it obtained access,” he told TechCrunch. “They say the evidence is consistent either with credentials/session data being taken without my knowledge, or with the account having been connected to an outside service.” In other words, a hacker was able to obtain access to De Swardt’s account and was covertly siphoning off his tokens. Because account support tracks total usage but not itemized usage, even upon request, this kind of theft could have gone on for months undetected. He posted his experience on Reddit and after 80 comments, he discovered he was not alone. One person claimed that their account “was auto-upgraded without my consent, my credit card got charged, and the usage shot from 0% to 100% automatically without me even touching it.” Another saw usage go from 0 to 49% in 12 minutes, when all they had used it for was a couple of prompts and a web search. One Claude user said their account burned through its max tokens every day for three days without them using it at all; this person then created a GitHub report about it. Like with the Reddit post, other users shared similar experiences there, too. Two of them posted emails from Anthropic where the company had — to its credit — identified and warned them that their tokens were being stolen. “We have recently become aware of a bad actor that is using common infostealer malware to steal Claude login sessions from people’s computers, then using those login sessions to access Claude accounts and consume their usage,” the email read. Infostealers are a type of malware that installs itself on a user’s computer and steals saved passwords, session data, and login credentials. When Anthropic saw suspicious activity, it signed the users out, invalidated existing authorizations, issued some refunds, and warned them that they may have malware. The company also said the malware didn’t come from using Claude itself. Such malware can be picked up from many sources online, from downloading infected software to clicking on infected ads. Anthropic did not send De Swardt one of those emails. He insists he found no evidence that his computer was compromised and says he still has no way of determining how hackers gained access. De Swardt’s Claude account was reinstated after about two weeks. But the difficulty of getting speedy help for the matter, plus the lack of an itemized usage, soured him on Claude. He cancelled his subscription in favor of Cursor and its ability to use multiple models, including more affordable open source options. In his experience, these other models work as well as Claude. “It’s not that much different or better,” he said, adding that he can’t see going back “without [Anthropic] actually having resolved the issue in any way.” He says Anthropic still lacks tools that allow users to see what’s consuming their tokens. “I don’t think there’s any way that these people can protect themselves.” When asked for information on how users can identify misuse, Anthropic declined to comment. Cognition hits $48B valuation, signaling investors believe AI coding is far from a winner-take-all market

TechCrunch | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Cognition, the startup developing coding assistant Devin, announced it raised $2 billion at a $48 billion valuation. The round, which comes just four months after Cognition’s previous fundraise at a $26 billion valuation, was led by Andreessen Horowitz, Accel, Founders Fund, General Catalyst, and Avenir. The startup’s soaring valuation signals that VCs still see room for multiple major players to capture meaningful market share in AI coding, one of the technology’s most significant applications. Cognition said that since announcing its last fundraise in May, its annualized run-rate revenue has grown from $492 million to $900 million. While the startup didn’t explain how it calculates that run-rate revenue, the metric is usually defined as a month’s top line multiplied by 12. Cursor, another popular coding assistant, was in talks in April to raise capital at a $50 billion valuation before agreeing to sell to SpaceX for $60 billion later that month. At the time of the funding talks, Cursor’s annualized revenue had surpassed $2 billion. This means Cognition currently commands a higher revenue multiple than Cursor did in the spring. Cursor ultimately sold to SpaceX largely because it was severely compute-constrained, according to investors familiar with its financials. It is unclear whether Cognition will face similar compute shortages. Cognition leases an Nvidia server cluster that costs hundreds of millions of dollars annually, which could push its total cash burn to $800 million this year, The Information reported. Like Cursor did before joining SpaceX, Cognition is training its own model based on open source alternatives. Over time, reducing its reliance on expensive third-party models from OpenAI and Anthropic will help cut costs and bring the company closer to breakeven. Cognition is expected to reach $4 billion to $5 billion in annualized revenue by the end of 2026, according to The Information. By comparison, TechCrunch reported in the spring that Cursor was on track to surpass $6 billion by year-end. Still, it’s notable that a16z, a major backer of Cursor, which made a killing when it sold to SpaceX, is back to lead a round in a Cursor competitor. Founded in 2024 by math prodigy Scott Wu, Cognition counts Mercedes-Benz, NASA, Goldman Sachs, and Citi among its major enterprise customers. The Exploration Company nabs $450 million to challenge SpaceX
2026-09-08

The Exploration Company nabs $450 million to challenge SpaceX

TechCrunch | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt The queue to send objects into orbit is growing, but space infrastructure isn’t keeping up with demand. As the realization sinks in that Elon Musk’s SpaceX can’t be the sole answer, other companies are raising their hand — and money. These include The Exploration Company (TEC), which operates out of Germany, France, Luxembourg, Spain, and Italy, and which has raised $450 million in what it describes as “the largest-ever Series C by a European space company.” But the race is global, with U.S.-based rival Stoke Space currently raising a $1 billion Series E round. Rivalry aside, these rounds show that investors on both sides of the Atlantic are willing to back companies seeking to build reusable spacecraft. That’s a category pioneered by SpaceX, but that many players, both public and private, now believe it shouldn’t solely control as space gains strategic importance. These sovereignty-related concerns create extra tailwinds for an EU-headquartered company like TEC. Led by Hélène Huby, a French national who spent two decades at Airbus and ArianeGroup, the company intends to use its funding to build the foundation for a reusable European heavy-lift launcher offering an affordable alternative to SpaceX. SpaceX itself may have no other choice but to loosen its nascent monopoly on low-earth-orbit launches to prioritize its own payloads; reports suggest that its Falcon rockets are already increasingly reserved for its Starlink satellites, amid uncertainty as to what will happen when these get retired in favor of Starship. This creates demand that TEC won’t be able to meet for years, even if it sticks to its self-admittedly ambitious timeline. According to Atomico, “10 Nyx missions are booked,” with “more than $2 billion in contracts and commitments.” Some of these commitments come from institutional partners, including the European Space Agency, but also NASA. Its Series C was also transatlantic, with Atomico, and the EQT-managed Scaleup Europe Fund co-leading the round alongside Bessemer Venture Partners, whose partner Alex Ferrara will join TEC’s board of directors. This latest round also consolidates TEC’s status as a company mostly funded by private investors, and one that plans accordingly. In a press conference earlier today, Huby said that she’d love to see Europe add human space flight to its roadmap, but wouldn’t be able to convince VCs to spend $4 billion towards a crew capsule, “then wait 10 years.” So, TEC’s timeline is more pragmatic, but it is tight. Its most immediate focus is Nyx, a reusable capsule akin to SpaceX’s Dragon and capable of carrying cargo, then people. The company intends to get Nyx to dock with the soon-to-be-retired International Space Station and return safely to Earth by November 2028. According to Huby, funding from the Series C will stretch to also fund Storm, a rocket engine program paving the way for further developments, at least initially. “Where we need more money is to build a launch pad, and to build the rocket itself,” she told journalists. TEC wouldn’t be the first European commercial space company to launch a rocket — German startup Isar Aerospace crossed that milestone out of Norway last weekend. Huby praised the feat as “great news for the whole ecosystem,” but said that TEC intends to build a much bigger rocket than Isar’s expandable Spectrum vehicle. However, Isar also has “a much bigger” launch vehicle in its roadmap, Ars Technica reported — presumably for the same reason Huby gave as to why TEC does. “In the next five to 10 years, half of our broadband communication could come from space, data centers could be in space; […] and if this is the case, we need massive rockets and cadence.” That cadence will rely on reusability, which TEC plans to reach by using the same technology as SpaceX and Stoke: full-flow stage combustion “It’s the most difficult, but also the most efficient engine cycle in the world. It is the first time ever in Europe that a company develops such a rocket engine,” Huby said. TEC’s advances haven’t gone unnoticed in Europe, with French president Macron and the European Commission’s president Ursula von der Leyen both praising its Series C. It is also not a coincidence that it was disclosed on the eve of the International Space Summit in Paris, where Huby hinted that related announcements will be made. TechCrunch
Meta debuts its Muse AI agent. Will consumers trust it?
2026-09-08

Meta debuts its Muse AI agent. Will consumers trust it?

TechCrunch | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Less than two weeks after Meta agreed to a massive $18 billion multistate settlement in a lawsuit over social media’s consumer harms, the company announced its biggest bet on consumer AI to date — and one that requires significantly more trust than social media ever did. On Tuesday, the company introduced Muse, its new personal AI agent that helps consumers with everyday tasks and projects for users in the U.S. To use Muse, consumers will have to trust Meta with more of their personal information than ever before. The AI agent works by connecting to the user’s apps and services that are a part of everyday workflows, like email, calendars, payments, and other things the individual may regularly use, like apps for health and fitness, the smart home, dining, shopping, music and events, and more. The idea is a sizable bet on what comes after the ChatGPT era, where AI chatbots answered questions, served as sounding boards, or even became digital companions. Instead, Muse is focusing on AI that can actually do things for you. The company says the agent can do things like sending emails, booking travel, lowering bills, filling out forms, creating plans, turning recipe reels into grocery lists, sending party invitations, and making purchases, leveraging Link by Stripe for checkout. The latter offers purchase protections, which could potentially ease consumers’ fears of letting an AI check out on their behalf. (Shopify’s Shop Pay and 1Password integrations are also coming soon.) Muse’s users can decide which apps and services they want to connect, doing so one at a time, to make the opt-in nature of using Muse more transparent. The agent is powered by Meta’s AI model, Muse Spark, and ships with built-in connectors (pictured below) for several services, with plans to add more over time. If a service the user wants isn’t available but offers a public API, Muse can set up a connection using credentials the user provides. When no API is available, Muse can access the service via the browser instead. Muse will initially be available via the web at muse.ai, through apps on iOS and Android, and through chats in WhatsApp. It will soon also make its way to Meta’s AI glasses, the company says. It will be free to use, with subscription plans kicking in as usage increases, which is why Muse requires a payment card to get started. Two paid plans will be available at launch: Power at $20/month and Maximum at $100/month. Both of these subscriptions offer more Muse usage for handing off everyday tasks, though Meta believes most people will remain on the free tier. (The company says the app includes a usage meter that shows users what percentage of their usage they have left. It will also warn users when free usage runs out and present options to subscribe.) Like other AI agents, Muse will continue to work even after the user leaves the app. It will also improve over time by learning from the user’s conversations what’s important to them to make suggestions unprompted, Meta noted. The concept is not unique to Meta. The agentic era is now coming into its own, as larger companies and smaller startups alike are experimenting with how AI agents will make the most sense for consumers and can become integrated into people’s daily lives. Some have tried AI web browsers or services, like Gemini Spark or Claude Cowork, that can kick off various tasks on consumers’ behalf. Others are integrating AI into the chat applications consumers use the most, like Apple’s iMessage platform, SMS, and WhatsApp. Despite their usefulness, these powerful agents have forced consumers to wrestle with difficult questions about how much privacy they’re willing to give up. Many early testers of the AI assistant Instinct were shocked to see the app required a broad “perpetual and irrevocable” license to “access, use, host, cache, store, reproduce, transmit, display, publish, distribute, and modify” any of the user’s materials, including for training its AI models. Under the hood, Meta claims that Muse runs in its own “dedicated, secure computer with its own browser,” Muse Secure VM, which offers various privacy, safety, and security protections over customers’ data. The company says a separate Sentinel agent runs on that same virtual machine, but is kept apart from Muse at the system level. This means Muse won’t have visibility into people’s passwords or payment methods. Meta also claims that Muse doesn’t share people’s conversations or data with Meta’s ads systems. (These claims are explained in more detail in a technical post, also released today, but will require deeper investigation by security experts.) Despite Meta’s documentation of its security measures, it remains to be seen whether the company has enough consumer trust for its agent to be successful. As it stands, Meta has a history of proclaiming one thing and doing another. In 2011, for instance, the tech giant settled with the FTC over charges that it deceived consumers by making users’ private information public without their approval. In 2019, the FTC penalized Facebook in a then record-breaking $5 billion settlement over eight separate privacy-related violations. In 2023, the FTC charged Meta with violating a privacy order that was filed after the 2019 settlement. In terms of technical matters, Meta has also had some big missteps before, having discovered in 2019 a number of users’ passwords in readable formats, exposing people to potential hacks. The massive Cambridge Analytica data scandal, which saw Facebook data belonging to millions of consumers collected by a third-party without their consent, still lingers in some people’s minds, too. Meta has also been repeatedly hauled before Congress to testify on how it protected — or failed to protect — minors from harm. With Congress failing to act, Meta ultimately became the target of several related lawsuits, including the one Meta just settled with 29 states in August, a New Mexico lawsuit over harms for children, where Meta was ordered to pay $942 million, and the thousands of personal-injury and school-district cases that are still pending against multiple social media giants. To ease consumers’ fears, Meta not only talks in depth about its security promises, offering technical documentation and explanations. The company has also designed Muse in a way that would make consumers feel more connected with the agent itself. Users can customize Muse by giving it a name, picking out its avatar, and configuring its look and various settings that dictate how the agent communicates with them. Time will tell if this personal connection and the utility Muse provides are enough for consumers to once again trust Meta with their personal information. Why the U.K., Canada, and France Are Banning Trade With Israeli Settlements
2026-09-08

Why the U.K., Canada, and France Are Banning Trade With Israeli Settlements

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Secretary of State for Foreign, Commonwealth, and Development Affairs Ed Miliband arrives in Downing Street to attend weekly Cabinet meeting in London on Sept. 8, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Israel ordered the closure of the British consulate in East Jerusalem after the United Kingdom led a coordinated effort by 12 governments to restrict trade with Israeli settlements in the occupied West Bank. British Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband announced the import ban on all goods from Israeli settlements in the West Bank on Tuesday. “The British government agrees that there is ethnic cleansing of Palestinians in areas of the West Bank, perpetrated by settler terrorists,” he said during remarks at the House of Commons, referring to growing incidents of violence against Palestinians living in the West Bank. Eighty attacks carried out by Israeli settlers resulted in casualties or property damage between Aug. 11 and Aug. 24, according to the United Nations High Commission for Human Rights. More than 1,040 Palestinians have been injured in such incidents across 2026, it reports, and 79 Palestinians, including 19 children, have been killed by either Israeli military personnel or settlers so far this year. That adds to the escalating violence against Palestinians in the West Bank since the terror attacks led by Hamas on Oct. 7, 2023, which killed around 1,200 people and took more than 200 hostage. Since then, another 1,100 Palestinians have been killed in the West Bank. Miliband said that the Israeli government had too often “turned a blind eye.” Following the U.K.’s lead, Canada and France announced similar trade bans Tuesday. Denmark, Finland, Iceland, Ireland, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Spain, and Sweden also joined those three countries in backing national or European restrictions, although their individual commitments vary. In a joint statement, the 12 governments said Israel’s actions were undermining the prospect of a two-state solution. The statement calls for a halt on settlement expansions and accountability for settler violence. The planned sanctions will ban the import of any goods from Israeli settlements in the West Bank, but will not impact any goods from Israel. Annual trade between the U.K. and Israel totaled around £6 billion ($8.1 billion) in the four quarters leading to Q2 2025, with goods imported by the U.K. from Israel valued at £1.2 billion ($1.6 billion). It is unclear how much of this trade originates from Israeli settlements in the West Bank. Chris Doyle, director of the Council for Arab-British Understanding, says that the settler economy produces mainly produce. “You see a lot of exports of agricultural goods like dates and avocados to Europe. You also see a lot of export of herbs, so this is in part the basis of the economy,” he says. A report published by Global Echo in June found that over 17% of the agricultural shipments to Europe that it analyzed contained settlement products. Doyle also says some settlements have been expanding industrial zones which “contribute to the production of many different types of Israeli exports.” And while the sanctions will not target goods from Israel, Doyle says that some products originating from settlements are instead labeled as originating from Israel. European policy has previously sought clarity on the exact origin of Israeli products and whether goods have come from West Bank settlements. “It's incumbent, therefore, on Israel to stop this, to bring itself in line with international law, and not export these products,” said Doyle in light of Tuesday’s sanctions. But Hirschhorn argues that given how intertwined the economies of settlements and Israel are, it is difficult and unrealistic to separate the two. “The lid to the container of hummus that's made in the settlement of Ariel is filled by hummus itself that was pumped out of a tube in the city of Ashkelon,” she suggests as an example. “There's no real way necessarily to separate the goods here from the goods there,” she tells TIME, arguing that Tuesday’s sanctions will ultimately impact Israeli businesses beyond the initial scope of the ban. “I’m not sure how an economic boycott of settler goods, no matter how specific or how tailored, would really achieve the policy goals that ultimately are desired here, which is the two-state solution,” she says. Despite the potential unintended impact sanctions could have, Doyle argues that this is down to the Israeli government to resolve. “It’s up to the state of Israel to uncouple its economy from the settlement economy,” he says. “Settlements do not lie within the sovereign borders of the territory of the State of Israel.” Under Article 49 of the Fourth Geneva Convention, the individual or mass forcible transfers, as well as deportations of protected persons from occupied territory, are illegal. It is also illegal for an occupying power to deport or transfer parts of its own civilian population into the territory it occupies. In light of Article 49, and in line with its view that Israel occupies the West Bank, the U.N. deems settlements in the West Bank illegal. Miliband reaffirmed Tuesday that the U.K. observes this position on the matter, as well. “To those who finance or facilitate illegal settlements, let me say this: You will face the full force of U.K. sanctions,” said the Foreign Secretary, outlining that action will be taken against those  who provide construction or financial support for expansions. There are an estimated 620,000 Israeli settlers living across the West Bank, as well as in East Jerusalem, according to B’Tselem, the Israeli Information Center for Human Rights in the Occupied Territories. Settlers “live on occupied territory, using resources, particularly water, which is so crucial that they're not entitled to, and they do that with the blessing, encouragement, and active support of the Israeli government,” says Doyle. The Israeli government has also been enabling and in some instances supporting the expansion of settlements across the West Bank. Last month, it opened bidding for more than 1,200 homes in E1, a largely undeveloped area between East Jerusalem and the existing Israeli settlement of Ma’ale Adumim. The broader plan for roughly 3,400 homes was approved last year and championed by far-right Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich, who has previously been sanctioned by the British government and others. Critics say that construction would connect Ma’ale Adumim more closely with Jerusalem while disrupting Palestinian territorial continuity between East Jerusalem and the northern and southern West Bank—further undermining the prospects for a viable two-state solution. Supporters say that the sanctions are significant because they turn longstanding international condemnation of Israeli settlements into economic action. “There is a line drawn here that many actors in the international community are now prepared to say, ‘We're going to back up the criticism with action,’ and that ‘Israel, you have to stop massive expansion of settlers,’” Doyle says. Others question whether broad restrictions on settlement trade effectively address the violence used to justify them. “The sanctions first and foremost don't target the perpetrators of settler violence specifically,” says Sara Yael Hirschhorn, a professor of history and American Jewish studies at the University of Haifa, suggesting that only a small number of settlers actually carry out such violence. Hirschhorn also notes that the timing of the measures, weeks before Israeli elections, could embolden far-right parties and hinder discussions around settler violence. “All it does is stir the pot in the midst of an election campaign, and strengthens the Israeli right, rather than approaching these issues with more subtlety when a new Israeli government had been elected,” she says. Israeli President Isaac Herzog warned that British sanctions on Israeli settlements “will prove to be a grave miscalculation.” “Sanctions are not a solution—dialogue is,” said Herzog. “Step away from the dead-end of sanctions and boycotts, and turn toward constructive dialogue and toward cooperation.” Far-right National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir also called on the British consulate in East Jerusalem, that works with the Palestinian Authority, to be closed. “This must be Israel's resolute response to British audacity,” he said. Mike Huckabee, the U.S. ambassador to Israel, said he did not know what “sanctions, repercussions or retaliations” might follow from Washington in response to the U.K. “I don’t know if they’re really aware of the implications and how it’s going to affect Brits,” he said, pointing toward legislation in 38 states that restricts government contracts or investments involving companies that boycott Israel. Asked about the sanctions, Secretary of State Marco Rubio told reporters: “Obviously we're not gonna do what the U.K. did.” “We don’t want to see anything destabilizing going on in the West Bank with so much going on in the region, we think it has implications on our ability to make progress with regards to Gaza,” Rubio continued. President Donald Trump is yet to publicly address the sanctions. When asked for comment on the matter, the White House directed TIME to Rubio's remarks. Alongside the closure of the British consulate in Jerusalem, Israeli Foreign Minister Gideon Saar  confirmed the removal of British representatives from the International Gaza Support Center, which coordinates relief efforts. Israel has also suspended the British training of Palestinian Authority forces in the West Bank and it has barred an NGO director and 11 elected representatives in the U.K. from entering Israel. Among those denied entry is Labour lawmaker Richard Burgon, who has backed accusations of genocide put against Israel for its conduct in Gaza. “The decision by the Israeli government to sanction me today will not intimidate me into silence,” said Burgon in response to the move. “I will continue to do what is right, speak out in defense of international law and stand up for justice for the Palestinian people.”

Yes, There Has Been an Uptick in High-Profile Art Heists. Here’s Why
2026-09-08

Yes, There Has Been an Uptick in High-Profile Art Heists. Here’s Why

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Police officers outside the Louvre Museum in Paris, France, on Oct. 27, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Nearly a year after precious jewels were stolen from the Louvre in broad daylight, France’s museums suffered another loss of major art when thieves stole four valuable paintings from the Renoir Museum on the French Riviera on Tuesday. These events, while seemingly isolated, point toward a broader trend in art heists across Europe. According to art theft investigator Anthony Amore, the crime has persisted throughout history. “It has always happened. It always will happen,” he tells TIME. At the same time, it’s “hard to deny” that there has been an uptick in major thefts of art from museums across Europe, he says. “In terms of big name artists who are targeted, the number of heists—successful heists—that have happened, is without question spiking over the last 11 months,” Amore says. That recent concentration was particularly striking in France. The country’s specialist art-crime office has recorded roughly 20 museum thefts annually over the past 15 years. But seven museums were targeted in September and October 2025 alone, the office’s chief told French lawmakers, describing an accumulation of unusually serious crimes. Read More: 25 Works of Art That Define America Now The latest heist targeted the former home and studio of Pierre-Auguste Renoir, an Impressionist artist born in the mid-19th century. The residence in Cagnes-sur-Mer was converted into a museum in 1960. French investigators have launched a national search for the perpetrators, who broke in before 6 a.m. and executed the heist in less than three minutes. Cagnes-sur-Mer Mayor Bryan Masson told press that the thieves were “well-equipped” and “well-informed,” slicing through the garden fence with what is believed to be either an electric knife or saw, entering the museum on the ground floor, and cutting the cords holding the artworks. Circumstances of their escape caused them to abandon two of the four pilfered artworks, but they fled with “Portrait of Madame Colonna Romano” and “Young Woman at the Well.” The incident bears similarities to the high-profile theft of the roughly $100 million worth of historic French crown jewels from the Louvre on Oct. 19, 2025. The haul has yet to be recovered or returned to the museum, even though authorities believe they have captured the entire four-man team responsible. Many of the heists in the year since have duplicated that team’s successful approach: Do it quickly and with force. Amore says that these “quick heists” often utilize brute force entry, like breaking a window. “Or it’s more of a smash and grab: Pull something off the wall and run. That's what we saw here,” he continues. “That's what we saw at the Louvre.” The approach often facilitates one of the most essential elements of a successful escape: time. “We're now getting into what I call the four-minute heist, where you can smash and grab your way into almost any museum in that amount of time,” says Christopher Marinello, the founder of Art Recovery International. After the Louvre, thieves now see museums as accessible targets, he says—as long as they act fast. “Criminals have discovered that with high-security museum systems, where the police are alerted and they arrive within four to five minutes, if they can get out before that, then they’re good—especially in a museum in a big city where you can use a moped or a scooter, and you could wind in and out of traffic,” Marinello continues. Brute force allows criminals to enter and exit the scene as fast as possible. For example, a gang used a tractor to batter down a gate at the Magnani Rocca Foundation, a private museum about 12 miles from Parma, Italy, on March 22. In just three minutes, thieves managed to steal paintings by Renoir, Paul Cézanne, and Henri Matisse. Thieves successfully undertook another heist lasting just a few minutes at a small museum in Sicily on Aug. 16, making off with several works by Renaissance painter Antonello da Messina. Not all thefts get the same level of publicity, and examples from the past year abound. Ancient Romanian artifacts were taken from the Drents Museum in the Netherlands in January 2025, $11 million of porcelain works were lifted from the Adrien Dubouché National Museum in France in September 2025, and a 200-carat diamond-and-platinum necklace from Vienna's Museum of Applied Arts (MAK), worth $4 million, was taken last month. And the problem has spread beyond Europe, as well. In the United States, more than 1,000 historic artifacts were stolen from an offsite storage facility belonging to the Oakland Museum of California in October 2025. The Sept. 8 Renoir heist is consistent with the methods used at the Louvre, says Amore. It is also consistent with the methods of most of the major art heists since early 2025. The similar methodologies point to potential cases of copycats, according to Amore. “My educated opinion is that I think the Louvre heist might have captured the imagination of thieves,” he says. Nick O’Donnell, a lawyer specializing in art and cultural property law, says that the Louvre heist potentially showed prospective thieves that if four thieves could successfully steal from one of the most famous museums in Europe, then they could easily transfer those tactics to less prominent museums. “It has given fuel to the perception that there are many museums where security may not be as tight as people think it is,” he says. “It seems to me that once that perception gets going for the would-be thieves, it's very hard to contain.” Marinello believes that a lack of security can be traced back to financial constraints. “They don't have money to protect their own treasures,” Marinello says. “And security systems are not keeping up with technology.” He also believes broader economic and political factors play into potential budget shortfalls. “The arts are always the first to go when governments are trying to save money,” he says. France, for example, cut its national culture budget by €150 million (about $170 million) in 2025, including a €200 million (about $226 million) reduction in heritage funding, which supports museums as well as monuments, archaeology, and archives. It is not directly evident how this might have impacted security at any of the museums robbed in the ensuing year; however, France launched a €30 million ($34 million) museum-security fund in July 2026, according to the French Culture Ministry. But it’s not all down to budget, Amore says. Part of the issue with upping security at cultural institutions is that it risks undermining the entire point of museums: bringing the public closer to incredibly valuable and precious artifacts. “The whole point of the museum is accessibility to rare valuables, important pieces, cultural patrimony,” Amore says. “Museums work really hard to get people to come and look at them and stand in front of them and take all the time in the world they need.” He adds: “The best a museum can do is try to make it difficult to get in, difficult to get back out, using layers of security to slow people down.” But the uptick in heists is not entirely explained by copycats and opportunists. The break-ins are also occurring amid high demand for precious metals and gemstones, which can make objects containing those materials attractive targets. They also come amid global economic instability, when some investors turn to art to hedge against inflation and diversify from stocks and bonds. A survey by Art Basel and UBS found that high-net-worth collectors allocated an average of 20% of their wealth to art in 2025, up from 15% in 2024. Europol, the law enforcement agency of the European Union, released a report in August highlighting how art thieves “are increasingly focusing on precious metals, gemstones, and culturally significant artefacts, driven by high market demand and ease of illicit resale.” Amore says those items may be less traceable and easier to fence than other valuables. Thieves can theoretically melt down the precious metals, remove gemstones from their settings, and sell the components separately. “People might be moving toward objects like jewels or highly valuable jewelry pieces because they think that they can sell them—or break them up and sell them more easily,” he says. But while that might explain the incidents at the Louvre and Vienna’s MAK, it doesn’t quite fit with paintings—which are easily identified and therefore significantly harder to offload. Famous paintings can be complicated to sell, in part because of how recognizable they are—which is intrinsic to their value. Marinello says that the thieves involved in the Renoir heist will soon find that the paintings they hold are “radioactive.” “And when they can't sell them, sometimes they will try to ransom them back to the museum,” he says. “And after they fail at all those other attempts, then the artwork will travel on the dark web at a fraction of its true value.” The risk may not be worth the consequences. Many of the culprits of recent high-profile heists have been caught by law enforcement, although recovering the lost art is another matter that has proven more difficult. In addition to the four people arrested in connection to the Louvre robbery, five people were detained last month in connection to the heist at the Magnani Rocca Foundation. At the same time, law enforcement and museum organizations have only grown more aggressive in their attempts to deter thefts and counteract the art heist trend. The International Council of Museums (ICOM) announced a new initiative with Interpol last year in hopes of supporting museums “in strengthening their security systems and reinforcing collective efforts to prevent the theft and illicit movement of cultural goods.” “The growing number and sophistication of security challenges facing museums and cultural institutions require urgent, coordinated, and informed action,” Medea Ekner, the director general of ICOM, said in a statement sent to TIME. “ICOM and INTERPOL are working closely to assess the scale and evolving nature of this threat, including through a sector-wide survey, and to identify where the museum community most needs support.”

We Must Defend the Refugee Convention
2026-09-08

We Must Defend the Refugee Convention

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In 1941, as the Nazis swept through Greece and took Athens, my grandfather, Nicolas Taptiklis, fled for his life. In the middle of the night, he boarded a tiny boat with twelve others and crossed the dangerous waters of the Aegean Sea to Turkey. Under Nazi occupation, his service with the British Navy made staying in Greece a risk he could not take. Greeks who helped the Allies risked arrest, imprisonment, or execution, and the punishment could reach their families and communities too. From Turkey, he made his way with thousands of others to Syria, where he finally found safety in Damascus. That journey never left him. When the war ended, Europe was filled with people who had also lost their homes, their families and, in many cases, nearly their lives. Around 11 million people were displaced in Germany: Holocaust survivors, former prisoners of war, forced laborers, and others left in camps, waiting for the world to decide what came next. My grandfather returned to Europe and put his medical training to work for people whose own lives had been broken by the same war he had fled. He joined the UN Relief and Rehabilitation Administration, one of the first humanitarian agencies and a precursor to UNHCR, the UN Refugee Agency. In a camp in Göttingen, Germany, he treated tuberculosis, typhoid, and people whose bodies had survived the war but still bore its scars. The world had learned, at unbearable human cost, what happens when people fleeing war have nowhere safe to go. Through WWII, Families were turned away from protection, people returned to countries where they feared for their lives, and millions were left in camps because home was no longer safe or even existed at all. From that devastation came a simple lesson; people escaping war and conflict should be able to find safety. They should not be forced back to a place where they could be killed or tortured. When people flee for their lives, they need somewhere safe to go. That principle became the heart of the 1951 Refugee Convention, adopted in the aftermath of the Second World War as countries tried to create a framework to prevent the atrocities of the war from ever occurring again. The Convention defined who is a refugee, set out the basic rights for refugees, and established the responsibilities of states toward people seeking protection. But more than a legal document, it was a promise of safety. A promise that, for the past 75 years, has saved millions of lives and stands as proof that even in our darkest hours, we can choose to protect one another. Even the clearest lessons of history can fade. A world of indifference, even cruelty, is creeping back. Xenophobia is rising. Humanitarian aid is shrinking. International solidarity is weakening. And once again, the promise of safety is under strain. As world leaders gather in New York for the United Nations General Assembly, we must reaffirm that promise. In times like these, we must hold fast to our promise and advocate for the continued need and lifesaving work of humanitarian agencies, like UNHCR, around the world. This is not only the morally right thing to do; it is also the practical one. When refugees are protected, communities are stronger, instability is reduced, and fewer people are forced to flee. A world that protects people in their greatest moment of need is a safer and more humane world for everyone. My grandfather experienced the generosity and compassion of the Syrians who sheltered him, but he also knew what a world of chaos and violence was like, and what its consequences were if there was no safety net of international protection. If my grandfather were alive today, he would be sounding a warning. He would be speaking up in defense of the Convention, of humanitarian action, of compassion. He knew all too well what it was like to flee in fear for your life. He saw how lives could be lost or shattered if there was no one to step in and protect them. I think of my grandfather on that boat, making that journey. I sound the warning on his behalf. To remind people that becoming a refugee can happen to any of us at any time. The right to seek safety was a promise made for us all, and that promise needs protecting and defending.
