Why Reading People's Confessions Online Feels So Good
2026-09-08

Why Reading People's Confessions Online Feels So Good

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In January 2026, a new community popped up on Reddit, the online message board site. Called /r/GirlDinnerDiaries, it now has a sizable 6 million members and invites people to post a photo of a meal—whether it’s something you’re eating while you post or another meal that gets at your state of mind—along with an anonymous blurb venting or soliciting advice.  "My partner only feels disgust at the idea of me as a mom," reads one. "I feel like it's never going to be OK," says another. The point is to activate the feeling of sharing over food, but over the internet. The snapshots are messy, yet evocative: French fries consumed in a nightclub, a breakfast burrito in a diner, yesterday’s lunch eaten hastily over a keyboard with a plastic fork. The posts wax poetic about the fate of marriages and the bizarre serendipity of life. Reading these dishy dispatches feels like staying up late with friends you rarely see, working over all that's gone down in your life in search of some meaning. Why are millions of people so captivated by these nameless confessionals served with a side of candid, unposed food photography? Scientists who study self-disclosure—the act of sharing personal, sensitive information with others—have some thoughts about why we share our deepest, darkest secrets on the internet. Talking about yourself feels pleasurable, and there’s science to back that up, says Leslie John, a professor of business administration at Harvard Business School and author of the book Revealing. She points to experiments conducted by psychologists Diana Tamir and Jason Mitchell, where people having their brains scanned in an fMRI machine answered either normal trivia questions or questions about themselves. “They found that in the ones that got to self-disclose, the pleasure centers of the brain were activated,” says John. “That's pretty powerful.” Sharing feels good because it’s necessary for human social interaction, John speculates. “When you self-disclose, when you take the social risk—and it really is a social risk—of sharing something a little bit personal, a little sensitive, you're modeling that you trust the recipient. That gesture is implicitly saying, ‘Hey, I trust you not to make a fool out of me; otherwise, I wouldn't be sharing this.’ And when you do that, it causes the other person to trust you back. So trust begets trust,” she says. In fact, John’s work has found that people are more interested in dating a hypothetical person who admits that they’ve done something terrible than someone who declines to answer the question. “It's better sometimes to admit the worst possible thing than to conspicuously abstain,” she says. Posting a photo of a paper plate with yesterday’s messy BBQ and a brownie you ate while processing getting fired is, in the age of the perfectly lit Instagram plate, a similar kind of move. “It's super relatable,” says John. “Showing things that are unvarnished is kind of paradoxically attractive.” Psychologists and sociologists used to think of self-disclosure mainly in the sense of sharing with people you know in real life, says sociologist Tamar Ashuri of Tel Aviv University. “The original studies back in the ’50s and ’60s on self-disclosure focus on face-to-face interactions,” she says. “In intimate relationships, people disclose, and it’s kind of an ultimate goal. You can't sustain an intimate relationship if you don't.” Another situation where people might self-disclose is the stranger-on-a-train context. It’s also face-to-face, but there is no lasting link, so the risks of disclosure are lower. But the arrival of the internet, and social media especially, produced new forms of self-disclosure that scientists are still working to characterize and understand. In the case of /r/GirlDinnerDiaries, says Ashuri, posters are anonymously disclosing intimate thoughts in a public forum, stranger-on-a-train style, with no face-to-face interaction, and yet it is still somehow gratifying. That combination emerged almost by accident, says the subreddit’s founder, a young woman named Taylor, who asked to be identified only by her first name. She had been posting frequently in another community, called Girl Dinner, and noticed a split: Some people wanted to know the slice-of-life stories behind the meals, while others only wanted to see the food. She created Girl Dinner Diaries as an overflow space for people who wanted both—and it took off almost immediately. “One of my favorite things is going out with the girls for some drinks and some appys and venting, getting it all off my chest,” Taylor says. Girl Dinner Diaries offered a place to do that without leaving the couch. Not every diary is wrenching. Taylor’s favorite post is a happy one: A woman woke up to discover that her husband had created a scavenger hunt for her, then shared a photo of the pasta with burrata they ate that evening. “It looked quite fancy,” Taylor says. “It looked very delicious.” Whether the story is heartwarming or devastating, the meal is an essential part of the formula. “Food provides a very positive, ordinary, culturally familiar medium,” Ashuri says. “But in this case, and this is what makes it so special, it allows people to express extraordinary issues…The food makes the extraordinary feel more ordinary.” She gives the example of one recent post, in which a woman posted a birthday cake and revealed that she and her son had celebrated his birthday in a domestic violence shelter. “I think this combination is extremely significant,” she says. The setup allows posters to reveal what are sometimes extreme experiences to a community gathered around a shared sensibility—with the unifying force of a leftover chimichanga presiding over it all. Food helps make the extreme relatable. The subreddit also tightly controls who can enter the conversation: Only approved commenters are allowed to respond, adding a layer of protection to a forum built around unusually intimate disclosures. There’s a happy medium to everything—don’t, perhaps, take to the web with every mental dip or life hiccup. And sharing online may not bring the same benefits as sharing in real life, cautions John. “Social media in so many ways facilitates self-disclosure,” she says. “But in many ways this medium doesn't give you the benefit you're looking for (e.g., real social bonding, connectedness).” Indeed, research suggests that people who post to social media every day tend to score worse on measures of mental health. Posting online can give easy access to reassurance from others, but this behavior can sometimes become destructive—if we don’t get the reassurance we seek, that’s a big blow, and constant reassurance may not help in solving the larger problems. But John thinks that in general, offline, we overestimate the harms of oversharing, while underestimating the costs of staying silent. The impulse to put an experience into words may help even when no one else reads them. Some of social psychologist James Pennebaker’s research suggests that privately writing about traumatic events can provide significant psychological relief, and even boost the immune systems of some patients. John never really applied her findings about disclosure to her own life, though, until she was working on her book about sharing. “Every time in my life that I could think of where I overshared, where I felt that cringe of TMI, it sucked in the moment. But every single time there was something amazing and redeeming in the long haul about it,” she says. “That made me think, ‘Oh wow, the decision we're making is systematically biased’…You get immediate negative social feedback, but there are benefits too. People trust you, people like you, people confide in you. Those come down the road.”

Want to Fall Asleep Faster? Write a To-Do List Before Bed
2026-09-08

Want to Fall Asleep Faster? Write a To-Do List Before Bed

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Nothing deters sleep like a nighttime mental ambush. Just as your head hits the pillow, tomorrow’s to-do list crawls out from under the bed. You’re suddenly answering emails in your mind, crafting meeting agendas, and ruminating over the appointment you still need to schedule. Try this instead: Write down tomorrow’s to-do list tonight. “One of the most common things I hear from patients with insomnia is, ‘I’m tired and exhausted, but my mind won’t shut off,’” says Dr. M. Taruj Ali, medical director of sleep medicine at Mary Washington Healthcare in Fredericksburg, Va. His patients often describe “a running mental inventory of unfinished tasks and what needs to happen tomorrow.” Spending a few minutes putting those thoughts on paper can give your brain permission to set them aside for the night. The timing matters, however: Don’t save the exercise for the moment you climb into bed. Experts recommend spending five to 10 minutes on it in the evening when you wrap up your structured tasks, like finishing work, chores, or whatever occupies the bulk of your time. “Getting those things out of your head and onto paper can reduce the pressure to keep thinking about them,” says Sari Chait, a clinical psychologist in Dedham, Mass. If a forgotten task pops into your head later, jot it on a notepad by your bed without turning it into a full-blown planning session. Here’s how to create a list that quiets your mind rather than revving it up. There’s some evidence that the habit can improve sleep. In a small 2018 study, researchers randomly assigned 57 adults ages 18 to 30 to spend five minutes at bedtime either writing about tasks they needed to complete over the next few days or listing what they had accomplished during the previous few days. The to-do-list group fell asleep an average of nine minutes faster—and the more specific their lists were, the faster they drifted off. That doesn’t mean a nightly list cures insomnia (or that reflecting on your accomplishments is a waste of time). The study included only healthy young adults who spent just one night in a sleep lab, and the researchers noted that they didn’t know whether the results would extend to people with insomnia. Still, it supports the idea that writing down unfinished tasks helps put them to rest. This strategy is a form of cognitive offloading: using an external tool—in this case, a physical to-do list—to reduce how much information your brain has to keep track of. Stephanie High, a psychology doctoral candidate in Spokane, Wash., thinks of every unresolved task as a browser tab running in the background of your brain. “You have all these tabs, you have all these apps open, and there’s always some activity in the background,” she says. Even when you’re not actively looking at one, it’s using a little battery and memory. Writing the task down allows you to acknowledge that it requires your attention—and then set it aside. “I know that I need to put my attention on this,” she says. “But I don’t need to do it right this second.” The benefits extend beyond bedtime. Planning tomorrow before you wrap up today creates a clear dividing line between work and the rest of your life, says Alexis Haselberger, a time-management and productivity coach in San Francisco. “It helps you disconnect, mentally, from work at the end of the day,” she says, rather than running through your responsibilities while you’re trying to talk to your kids, catch up with your partner, or watch TV. There’s a morning payoff, too: “You have a list, so instead of spending 30 minutes getting into your day, you can just start.” If your current organizational system could most accurately be described as “chaos,” Chait suggests starting with one big brain dump: “Get it all out on paper, and then you can organize it from there,” she says. Once you’ve made that initial list, the daily exercise can be much smaller. At the end of each workday, choose three priorities for tomorrow. Next, compare those priorities with your calendar. “If you have five meetings tomorrow, you’re probably not going to be able to make much headway on that big project,” Haselberger says. Move anything that won’t fit to another day. The goal is to make a plan you can reasonably stick to—not compile an unrealistic wish list. Specificity matters, too. “‘Prep for meeting’ leaves the loop half-open because your mind still has to hold on to what ‘prep’ means,” High says. “‘Pull Q3 numbers, email the deck’ closes the loop, because there’s nothing it has to remember.” The same goes for the difference between “call Mom” and “call Mom about Thursday’s appointment.” Include enough detail that when you wake up, you’ll know exactly what you meant. You don’t, however, need to produce a display-worthy masterpiece. “It doesn’t have to be perfect,” Chait says. “It doesn’t have to be pretty.” The sleep study used pen and paper, but it didn’t test whether handwriting works better than typing. Chait says a notebook or a notes app on your phone will do the trick; the best method is the one you’ll actually use. For some people, writing a to-do list can increase anxiety, especially if it morphs into a tedious planning session or an inventory of everything you failed to accomplish. If the page starts filling up with every unresolved problem in your life, it can feel less like a plan and more like self-flagellation. “You’ve just handed your nervous system a threat list right when you’re trying to wind down,” High says. Keep the exercise brief, and focus on what you’ll do next rather than dwelling on what went wrong that day. The goal isn’t to plan out your entire life. It’s to spend five or 10 minutes capturing what needs to be remembered—and then disengage from planning until the next day. If another task pops into your head later, write it down—but stop there. Chait keeps a pen and paper next to the bed for exactly that reason. You don’t want to spend time processing your feelings or actually acting on the thoughts, like writing an email,” she says. “You simply want to write down what the thought or to-do is.” Then put the paper away. Finally, treat the habit like an experiment, not a new obligation. “It only works if it works for you,” High says. Try it for a few days, notice whether you feel calmer at night and more focused in the morning, and adjust how much detail you include. Otherwise, she points out, you risk opening “another tab of how I need to close more tabs”—when the whole point is to give your brain less to do.

The Growing Push to Ban Superintelligent AI
2026-09-08

The Growing Push to Ban Superintelligent AI

TIME | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Professor Stuart Russell addresses U.K. lawmakers about the risks of superintelligent AI at an event in Westminster, London, on Sept. 7. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings After a summer of cyberattacks sent AI rocketing up the political agenda, bills seeking to ban so-called superintelligent AI are gaining momentum on both sides of the Atlantic. Last week, Senator Bernie Sanders announced plans to introduce the Ban Artificial Superintelligence Act, which would prohibit the development of smarter-than-human AI systems and temporarily pause all other advanced AI research until new safety rules can be established. The bill is expected to be introduced in the coming weeks, according to Sanders’ office. And on Tuesday, British lawmaker Alex Sobel stood up in the House of Commons and introduced what he said was the first bill seeking to ban superintelligence in any G7 parliament. The bill defines superintelligent AI as a system that could disempower state authorities, and seeks to give the state new powers to monitor and restrict systems that may be “precursors” to superintelligence. Both the U.S. and U.K. bills additionally seek to compel each government to pursue a global treaty banning the development of superintelligent AI anywhere in the world. “A national bill does not solve the international problem where superintelligence can be developed somewhere else,” says Andrea Miotti, the founder of ControlAI, a campaign group that wrote the U.K. bill and consulted on the U.S. one. “The only way to address that is via international agreements between countries.” At an event in Westminster on Monday ahead of the British bill’s introduction, Stuart Russell, a professor of computer science at the University of California, Berkeley, issued a stark warning to a small audience including some U.K. lawmakers. “There's a significant chance of a Chernobyl-sized catastrophe” due to AI, he told them, “such as a coordinated sabotage of financial systems, communication systems, or electrical grids, that directly or indirectly would cost millions of lives and cause the economic collapse of many countries.” He characterized this grim possibility as a best-case scenario if AI development were to continue without government regulation. “The other possibility is a much larger catastrophe in which humanity loses control irreversibly, and we have no say over whether we continue to exist,” he said. “The third option, that things magically go well, is not realistic.” Though the two bills appear to be the first to address the specific policy of superintelligence, other bills in both legislatures seek to address AI risks in different ways. In July, Republican and Democratic lawmakers co-sponsored the AI Kill Switch Act, which seeks to require AI companies to maintain the capability of shutting down their increasingly autonomous AI systems. In the U.K., lawmakers recently attempted to amend a cybersecurity bill to include similar requirements. The efforts reflect a growing awareness among some lawmakers of what they see as the severe national security risks of AI companies developing superintelligent AI systems, particularly in light of the July disclosure of the Hugging Face incident, in which a swarm of OpenAI agents hacked a separate company without humans telling them to do so, or initially even being aware of their actions. But the passage of either bill is unlikely. Sanders is an independent senator, and his co-sponsor is a Democratic congressman. The Republican-majority Congress is unlikely to support the bill, which goes against the White House’s AI agenda. And although the lawmaker who introduced the British bill is a member of the governing Labour Party, the U.K. government has not supported the policy. It is a so-called “Ten Minute Rule bill,” a type of bill that rarely progresses past its first reading, meaning the possibility of it becoming law is vanishingly slim. ControlAI acknowledges these limitations, but says the bills are nevertheless a key step toward government regulation of AI. Superintelligence is “something that no company, no individual, no country knows how to keep under control … most countries don’t want that for very good reason,” says Miotti. “Fundamentally, as has happened in the past with other bills, the first introduction is just the beginning.” Another speaker who addressed British lawmakers on Monday was Daniel Kokotajlo, a former OpenAI employee who quit in 2024 over what he said was the company’s failure to properly address the extinction risks of advanced AI. “Things are really crazy over here in San Francisco,” he told lawmakers in London by video. “The core problem is that most of the world just doesn't yet believe that it's really going to happen,” he said, referring to the development of superintelligence. “Once people realize that these companies might actually build superintelligence in the next few years, I think the obvious conclusion of almost all people is going to be: they shouldn't be allowed to do that.” Kokotajlo, who has an established track record of making accurate predictions about AI progress years in advance, raised the possibility of AI agents taking substantial and covert control of infrastructure at AI companies, and using that foothold to establish a swarm of AIs that could resist human attempts to shut it down. “It seems like if we had just gotten a little bit less lucky, and the AIs had been a bit smarter, then we still wouldn't know about it [the Hugging Face incident], and there would be this persistent autonomous AI adversary still at large inside OpenAI with the ability to compromise and grow itself further,” he said. “And then, as the next months and years go by, and OpenAI makes more powerful agents, those agents could be brought into the swarm.”

Medicine Needs to Get Serious About AI
2026-09-08

Medicine Needs to Get Serious About AI

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
For as long as ChatGPT has existed, Americans have been asking it, “Could that funny little spot on my toe be cancer?” And as AI has advanced over the past few years, doctors have begun to use specialized models to ask much the same questions in clinics. In the face of these developments, professional medical societies have presented a unified front: Doctors must always have the final say in clinical care. We disagree. As the medical societies allow, physicians’ enhancing their work with AI tools may be a perfectly good way to deliver medicine. (One of us—Khosla—has in fact invested in a primary-care start-up, co-founded by his son, in which doctors supervise AI assistants.) But for Americans to get the best health care possible, we need to be willing to experiment with models that put AI in control. This notion has caused a stir in the medical community. When we published a commentary in the Journal of the American Medical Association last month arguing that AI-only care could soon become superior to care provided by human physicians, the CEO of the American Medical Association denounced us as undermining the patient-doctor relationship. The organization’s position was unchanged: AI should be relegated to a supporting role in clinical care. It’s an argument that ignores the available evidence and could hold untold numbers of Americans back from better health. Much of modern medicine—including managing hypertension, determining the right treatment for many cancers, and addressing chemotherapy side effects—is largely algorithmic. A physician needs to ask a patient targeted questions, determine possible diagnoses, order tests to identify the correct one, offer a treatment plan, and adjust it depending on how the patient responds. That series of actions is steered by detailed guidelines from professional societies. Yet doctors—despite the best of intentions—make plenty of mistakes when following those steps. Studies indicate that up to 15 percent of American patients are misdiagnosed each year, leading to tens of thousands of preventable deaths. That’s why health-care providers have software that notifies them if they, say, prescribe a deadly dose of a drug. Any software that could practice medicine autonomously would be similarly specialized: not generic ChatGPT, but a model trained in medical knowledge with the appropriate guardrails to minimize risks and mistakes. Such a model could check for drug interactions more consistently and comprehensively than physicians. And it would be able to check in with patients much more frequently than human health-care providers do. Some AI models have already proved adept at such tasks. In one study published last year, expert physicians judged Google’s Articulate Medical Intelligence Explorer—a highly trained AI model for medical diagnosis and reasoning—as significantly better than human doctors at getting actors playing patients to open up. The “patients” revealed more about their complaints, along with their medical, family, and medication history, all of which can be crucial for an accurate diagnosis. In a 2023 study, patients with type 2 diabetes were randomized to have their insulin dose adjusted by physicians or autonomous AI. The machines got patients to a stable dose faster than human physicians, and their patients took their insulin more reliably and had less distress related to their diabetes. What about the physician-AI hybrids that medical associations are backing? Research on them is sparse, and more is needed. A 2025 review of 52 studies concluded that, on balance, such hybrids “neither outperformed medical AI alone nor surpassed the best of clinicians.” In at least a few of those studies, human-AI hybrids outperformed AI alone. But in those hybrid teams, the AI controlled the final clinical decision—the opposite of what the AMA and others have in mind. Medical organizations are not entirely closed-minded to the possibility of change. For example, last week the American College of Physicians published a position paper that allowed that “fully autonomous AI is possible,” but cautioned that its use should be infrequent and “limited to low-risk, low-complexity decisions, and always allow for bringing a clinician into the loop.” John Whyte, the CEO of the AMA, told The Atlantic that he was “disappointed” in the quality of the analysis in our JAMA commentary last month. He questioned the design of the studies we reviewed, many of which involved simulated situations with actors or a retrospective review of existing cases rather than real clinical outcomes, and did not follow patients for years or decades. We agree that longer-term, real-world studies are necessary; hopefully, once advanced AI medical tools have existed for more than a handful of years, results will begin to arrive. Whyte was enthusiastic about further study of physicians controlling AI tools, but only cautiously open to further exploration of autonomous AI in medicine, arguing that the latter would need to be highly regulated. Reasons exist, of course, to be skeptical of AI-driven medical care. In nine of the 51 studies we reviewed, human physicians outperformed the machines. But some of these tested older AI models or general-use AI models that weren’t trained on medical information and specific guardrails for patient care. Prominent physicians have also argued that real-life doctors are harried by 15-minute appointments and administrative burdens such as prior authorization. That is certainly true, but it is also irrelevant to many of the experiments we reviewed, which examined relatively hassle-free simulations of medical appointments. One of the most common objections to an AI-doctor future is that robots lack the human connection that patients need to build trust. The American College of Physicians’ recent position paper, for example, cited AI’s inability to make eye contact. But consider that doctors, too, can be robotic. In a Gallup survey conducted late last year, 21 percent of respondents said they had turned to AI because they had previously felt dismissed by a health-care provider. In the 2025 study of Google’s AMIE, patient actors reported that the AI was more emotionally supportive than the physicians were. In a systematic review of 15 studies comparing AI chatbots with physicians, 13 found that AI had significantly higher perceived empathy. Like human physicians, AI is not and never will be perfect. But isn’t the possibility of reducing fatalities caused by physician error—even by just a little—worth exploring? Instead of writing off a possible source of progress, the medical community should help answer the key questions raised by AI: How should researchers devise ethical, real-world situations to test autonomous AI models? How should governments and professional organizations regulate them? And how should medical education be reformed so that human doctors can continue to do what they uniquely can? Hundreds of years ago, doctors believed that disease was caused by an imbalance of “humors” in the body. Even after Louis Pasteur proved that bacteria caused infection and Joseph Lister introduced sterile surgical techniques, physicians cut patients to have them bleed out what ailed them, not bothering to clean the knife or their hands. But medicine progresses. Humans have learned from our past mistakes and created new drugs, procedures, and treatments to massively lengthen lifespan and improve quality of life. Today need not be any different. Doctors have been presented with a powerful tool that has the potential to improve care for millions of people. It’s their duty not to stand in the way.

Cereal Has Strayed Too Far
2026-09-08

Cereal Has Strayed Too Far

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Breakfast-cereal boxes are a window into both the politics of food and American culture. As a particularly timely example, since Robert F. Kennedy Jr. took office last year as secretary of health and human services, he has called for the removal of artificial dyes from breakfast cereals. On Fox News in 2024, he lamented that the bright colors in Froot Loops are “literally poisoning our kids.” (How exactly these dyes interact with human health is complicated.) Cereal companies responded right away. General Mills announced last month that it had transitioned its entire U.S. portfolio of cereals—including Trix and Lucky Charms—to all-natural dyes made from fruits, veggies, and spices. Kellogg had previously announced plans to do the same by the end of 2027. Target stopped selling any cereals with artificial colors earlier this year. For the Make America Healthy Again movement, the removal of artificial dyes is a big win. But cereal still comes with a major concern for many consumers. Nearly all ready-to-eat breakfast cereals sold in the United States—and especially those marketed to children—are presweetened. The initial loading of breakfast cereals with sugar has been attributed to a single person: Jim Rex, a heating-equipment salesman who lived in Pennsylvania in the early 20th century. As the story goes, Rex was appalled by the spoonfuls of sugar his kids added to their breakfast cereals and set out to create a version that would not induce such behavior. His brilliant contribution: a glossy honey coating on puffed wheat, a cereal he named Ranger Joe Wheat Honnies in 1939. It didn’t take long for Rex’s strategy to backfire. By 1949, Post had launched Sugar Crisp. Kellogg introduced Sugar Pops in 1951, Sugar Frosted Flakes in 1952, and Froot Loops in 1963, each among the company’s all-time best sellers. General Mills launched Sugar Smiles in 1953. The next year, the company added sugar and artificial colors to its Kix cereal, thus creating Trix. In 1950, such presweetened cereals represented just 5 percent of all cereals sold, but by the mid-1970s, kids accounted for almost 40 percent of all cereal consumption. In 2014, the Environmental Working Group estimated that kids could ingest about nine pounds of sugar a year just by eating a serving of cereal every day. Advocates for children’s health have long complained about the high sugar content of breakfast cereals. In the early 1970s, Congress held hearings to highlight cereal’s inadequate nutritional quality. Cereal companies objected, arguing, correctly, that grains are nutritious. But they also responded by trying to make their products appear less sugary. They began by removing the S-word from cereal names, and some substituted honey for sugar on the theory that people perceive honey as more wholesome and nutritious. In the 1990s, companies hit on another clever strategy: If they used multiple sugar types, they could list grains first on a product’s ingredients but sprinkle sugars throughout the list. Cereals looked healthier, but when Consumer Reports compared the sugars in 27 breakfast cereals in 2008, it found that many were still 50 percent sugar by weight. Rumors circulated that federal agencies were working to set nutrition standards for foods advertised on television programs aimed at children younger than 12, which would likely spell trouble for sugary cereals. In 2009, General Mills pledged to reduce sugar in its most popular kids’ cereals—most of which had at least 12 grams a serving—to single digits. Its pledge would apply to 10 cereals, including some of its most popular, such as Lucky Charms and Cocoa Puffs. The results were mixed. By 2012, General Mills had reached the single-digit goal for Cinnamon Toast Crunch, Cookie Crisp, and more. Some of this success was due to decreased serving size. And the best General Mills could do for Lucky Charms was 10 grams of sugar in a three-quarters-cup serving—which the company found to be the minimum sweet spot for kids’ acceptance. The project had encountered a harsh reality: Kids like sugar, and companies remove it at their peril. Sugary cereal is not just a consumer concern; it is also a matter of government nutrition policy. Since 1980, the federal Special Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants, and Children (WIC) has limited the sugars in cereals it will cover to six grams an ounce, about half of the amount in typical cereals aimed at kids. WIC can be a lucrative business for cereals that qualify, so the limit has induced companies to take two actions. The first is to formulate new cereals with less sugar specifically for the WIC market. In 2006, for example, General Mills introduced a new kid-targeted cereal, Dora the Explorer, “lightly sweetened cinnamon corn puffs,” with six grams of sugar in a 27-gram serving—an amount that meets the WIC standards. The second is to petition to change how sugar in cereals is counted. When the six-gram rule went into effect, in the early 1980s, Kellogg immediately objected. Its Bran Flakes met the WIC standards, but Raisin Bran did not; it contained 12 grams of sugar an ounce, seven from the raisins. Kellogg argued that raisins are healthy and pointed out that WIC was encouraging women to add raisins to their diet to increase their iron intake. For decades, despite Kellogg’s persistent efforts, the U.S. Department of Agriculture did not budge. But times change. In 2024, USDA issued its third comprehensive update to the WIC food package since 1975. The agency retained the six-gram limit but specified that it applied only to added, not total, sugars. It said the policy change was justified to align with listing “added sugars” on food labels and with the government publication Dietary Guidelines for Americans, and to create a “more permissive standard for products containing naturally occurring sugars.” The distinction between natural and added sugars has continued under Kennedy. The new dietary guidelines, released in January, declare that no amount of added sugar is recommended for healthy eating. At a press conference announcing the new guidelines, Kennedy told reporters, “Today, our government declares war on added sugar.” Current breakfast cereals have less sugar than they did years ago, but it’s hard to remove completely. Some amount of sugar is still needed to mask the bitter taste of added vitamins. Sugar also helps cereal flakes, puffs, and Os hold their shape in milk. Less sugar is better for kids’ health, but more is better for structural integrity—and taste. For a truly healthy cereal, look to a time before Jim Rex. The most nutritious option will have one ingredient: wheat or oats. Even if you add your own teaspoon of sugar, it will be only four grams. This article has been adapted from Marion Nestle and Lisa Sutherland’s new book, Sugar Coated. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

When Mothers Get Arrested for Mothering
2026-09-08

When Mothers Get Arrested for Mothering

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This past June, a Virginia mother named Karyann Parkinson took a bike ride with her 5-year-old son and stopped by a pond inside their gated community to collect goose feathers from the grass. The two of them rode back home, whereupon Parkinson’s son asked his mother if he could walk back to the pond by himself to fetch a few more feathers. Parkinson, who was eight months pregnant with her fifth child, studying for the bar exam, and—perhaps most pressing—making lunch, agreed. The walk was relatively short, along a sidewalk 10 feet from the road and across two intersections with crosswalks, which Parkinson knew her son could navigate successfully (he had done so six times that morning). But on his way, the boy was spotted by a concerned passerby, who notified a community security guard, who in turn notified the police. Before long, several police officers were at Parkinson’s door, followed by Child Protective Services agents. Officials in the case decided to press charges against Parkinson, and last week she was convicted of contributing to the delinquency of a minor and placed on Virginia’s child-abuse-and-neglect registry, where she will remain for seven years. Parkinson told me that her son—who competently walked the security guard back to their house after politely explaining that he wasn’t allowed to get into cars with strangers—had become distraught upon learning that he had apparently done something very bad. And although the police treated Parkinson as though she were negligent, her husband, who works as a marriage and family therapist and sees a lot of anxious young adults, tried to explain to the officers that they had made a deliberate choice to let their children encounter appropriate levels of challenge and risk. But the officials remained unmoved. Two weeks later, two police officers showed up at Parkinson’s door with a warrant for her arrest. “It just kept escalating,” she told me. “No one was taking a step back and going, Oh, this is fine. This is a fine situation.” She gave birth to her baby a week later. Last week’s guilty verdict, Parkinson said, has been disorienting. Having spent 15 years taking some pride in her mindful choices as a parent, she has lately been doubting herself—“and thinking, Am I really crazy?” Her lawyers have appealed the decision, and Parkinson will receive a new trial. People are having fewer children around the world for a variety of reasons, but some observers suggest that the steep expectations placed on parents—and mothers in particular—may be at least partly to blame for the fertility cliff in the United States. In response to a pervasive atmosphere of parental stress, a number of social-media influencers and public intellectuals have recommended that parents just do less—no more assiduously planned birthday extravaganzas, no more enrichment programs scheduled from breakfast to bedtime, no more hawkish monitoring to rescue children from any hint of danger or discomfort. But even though working parents may long for the days when children were more independent—a wish reinforced by countless editorials declaring the wisdom of letting children roam freely, away from their screens—society seems to have irrevocably changed. Most families now include two parents working full-time, and although many of these adults enjoyed the unchaperoned pleasures of a latchkey existence, the sight of children left to their own devices now horrifies not only the neighbors but also cops and prosecutors. In other words, parents are caught in a vise made up of financial demands and socially inflated expectations around parenting. Parkinson’s story is not unique. In 2008, Lenore Skenazy made national news when she published a column about letting her 9-year-old son ride the subway home after a trip to Bloomingdale’s in Midtown Manhattan, a decision that many people likewise characterized as a form of child abuse or neglect. Skenazy, who has since founded what she calls the Free-Range Kids movement, occasionally publishes stories about mothers who are arrested or locked up for letting their children enjoy what used to be considered ordinary childhood pleasures. In 2020, a Georgia mother of five was jailed for allowing her 14-year-old daughter to babysit her younger siblings during the pandemic, when schools and day-care facilities were closed but parents still had to work. In 2024, a mother of four in Georgia was handcuffed when her 10-year-old son was found walking into town, less than a mile from their home, while she was taking one of her other children to a doctor’s appointment. Parkinson’s story has become a national phenomenon in large part because Skenazy first called attention to it in Reason magazine. Raising children comes with enough trade-offs without enforcing unforgiving standards for parental attention, entertainment, and supervision. Notably, none of the cases in which police arrested mothers for their presumed negligence involved children who had been actually harmed. One hesitates to admit to such a taboo practice in print, but my own 10-year-old daughter has ventured out unaccompanied to hang out with her friends or pick up a few things from the store, and although I have fretted over getting pinched by the police, she treasures those opportunities to establish some independence even more than I appreciate time away from monitoring and enabling her various whims and desires. Mixing in activities that don’t require constant parental oversight is unfortunately a daring practice these days, but it seems crucial to balancing work and other adult responsibilities with the demands of raising children. This is not to say that allowing kids some independence comes without risks: Cars pose a threat, as does every child’s latent desire to engage in mischief. Unknown adults assailing children with ill intentions, though a terrifying prospect, is exceedingly rare; stranger abductions account for less than 1 percent of missing-child cases nationwide. But these risks may nevertheless be overstated in our social imagination because ours is a low-trust society; in other countries, young people are granted much more freedom because parents presume that the adults they encounter will be helpful and supportive. What Parkinson, Skenazy, and many other parents understand is that independence is good for children, who stand to build confidence and competence by doing things on their own. And fostering that independence is, in the end, the primary goal of parenting. The job is hard and bittersweet by design: Every step they take in the world is a step away from you. This, of course, is where their journeys are destined to lead.

Why Lonnie Bunch Is Leaving the Smithsonian
2026-09-08

Why Lonnie Bunch Is Leaving the Smithsonian

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
This story was updated at 6:12 p.m. ET on September 8, 2026. Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie Bunch plans to retire from the institution at the end of this year, bringing to a close a tenure marked by unprecedented pressure on the museum complex from a White House keen on controlling how to tell the nation’s history. After a person with knowledge of the situation confirmed the departure to The Atlantic, Bunch addressed Smithsonian employees at a town hall this afternoon, telling them, “I may be going away, but I’m still going to be a pain in the ass to some people.” The crowd of staffers, who spilled out of an auditorium and into an overflow room at the Hirshhorn Museum, clapped and cheered, even as some likely wondered: Why now? In conversations with intimates, Bunch, who became secretary in 2019, has been floating his retirement since at least the beginning of this year. But the increased attention from the White House surely made stepping down an even more delicate decision. Bunch, who is 73, tried to deflect any notion that his departure was Trump-related, telling staffers, “I now realize that I’m old as dirt, and it’s time for the next chapter in my life.” He also suggested that the timing may have been influenced by his wife, Maria Marable-Bunch, who recently retired from the National Museum of the American Indian. “The Smithsonian represents more than a career to me; it’s been an integral part of who I am,” he said. “I think about during the many years here, I met the love of my life, who’s retired and told me I better retire soon.” Bunch emphasized at the meeting and in an email to staff that his next chapter will allow him to “continue to protect the integrity of the Smithsonian and to champion the importance of a history that illuminates and symbolizes the complex story of our nation,” though he did not offer further details. One Smithsonian staffer, who requested anonymity because they were not authorized to speak on the record, told us that while “as a secretary, he has to be very polite and decorous,” Bunch can be noisier outside the institution. Still, that will likely come in a measured form, characteristic of Bunch. As the staffer put it: “He’s not a real bomb-thrower, usually.” The person described the secretary as an exceptionally popular leader among staff:  “He’s one of the rare people in Washington everyone seems to like. Well, except, you know, certain people, obviously.” Bunch’s exit will leave the Smithsonian, which is largely federally funded, with a vacancy at the top as it faces escalating efforts from the Trump administration to influence its work. Bunch, known to be diplomatic and evenhanded, had largely staved off the White House’s encroachment over a tumultuous 18 months in which the administration issued executive orders and letters criticizing the museums and threatened to withhold funding. In July, President Trump signed an executive order calling for signage to be added to the exterior of the National Museum of American History warning about what the administration claims is an inaccurate and partisan telling of history. Smithsonian leaders have countered that telling the American story fairly and accurately means discussing its complexities too. In a news release, Bunch said: “There is no place like the Smithsonian—it has been one of the great loves of my life, and I am extraordinarily proud of what we have accomplished together. I leave with pride and sadness, gratitude and joy, and with enormous confidence in the people who will carry this remarkable institution forward.” The chair of the Smithsonian Board of Regents, Franklin D. Raines, thanked Bunch for his service and said the institution’s governing body will be “focused on ensuring stability during this time of transition.” He said that the Smithsonian would soon announce an acting secretary and begin a search for Bunch’s successor. “There are some very strong internal candidates,” Kim Sajet, the former director of the National Portrait Gallery, told us today. Before becoming secretary of the entire Smithsonian, Bunch was the founding director of the National Museum of African American History and Culture, which has been widely praised for its powerful handling of the darkest chapters of America’s past. In Bunch, the Smithsonian had at its helm an advocate for its continued independence. The first historian and the first Black leader of the 21-museum complex, he spent nearly 38 years in roles across the institution. His departure has increased anxieties about the institution’s future. Read: First the Kennedy Center, now the Smithsonian “As the only Secretary of the Smithsonian to come from a background in history, he was well positioned to understand—and to defend—the discipline when history itself is under attack,” a former colleague of Bunch’s, who worked with him for years, texted us after the news. “His eloquent defense of history, grounded in a deeply rooted cultural understanding of the American story, will leave a leadership vacuum and the Smithsonian will be ever more vulnerable,” the colleague, who requested anonymity out of fear of reprisal from the administration, went on. In a way, Trump and Bunch exemplified a primary tension facing not only the Smithsonian in this moment but the field of American history more generally. Trump’s call for a feel-good, Founding Fathers–forward telling of American history is somewhat at odds with Bunch’s biggest achievements: the dramatic reevaluation of whose story has a place on the National Mall and how the country faces the harder parts of its past. Bunch will leave the institution just as it seeks to hold off a threat against the very work that defines his legacy. One of the most dramatic illustrations of that tension occurred in May 2025, when Trump announced that he was firing Sajet, even though he had no authority to do so. The Board of Regents affirmed Bunch’s authority over hiring and firing, though Sajet ultimately resigned from the National Portrait Gallery in June, saying that the dispute had become a distraction for the institution. Sajet, who now leads the Milwaukee Art Museum, told us she still misses the Smithsonian and has watched with dismay as the pressure that came for her first continues to bear down on her former colleagues. Through the changes, Sajet said that she and Bunch have remained in close touch. “I think it’s one of the loneliest jobs in the world to be in the head of a large institution like that,” she said. Sajet said Bunch’s background as both a historian and a museum director made him well suited to steer the Smithsonian through periods of turmoil—including the racial-justice reckoning after George Floyd’s murder and the pandemic. “I know that you believe that your legacy is building the National Museum of African American History and Culture,” Sajet recalled telling him. “But I’d like to suggest to you that you being the secretary at this particular moment in time is really important.” She said she believes those same qualities carried him through the Smithsonian’s showdowns with the White House, adding that he’s labored to maintain the institution’s unusual relationship with the federal government (all three branches are represented on the Board of Regents). “He’s tried very hard to maintain the respect for our elected officials, and I admire him for it,” Sajet said. Trump’s attacks at times seemed to directly target Bunch’s work. Last summer, the president came after the Smithsonian on social media, writing that it focused too much on “how bad Slavery was.” The two later met over lunch at the White House, a conversation that Bunch described as “the most stressful lunch I’ve ever had in my life.” In a December letter that the administration sent to the Smithsonian, the White House threatened to withhold funding if the museum complex did not comply with an order to send over a trove of documents about its work and comply with its demands to tell an adequately patriotic version of American history. Americans “will have no patience for any museum that is diffident about America’s founding or otherwise uncomfortable conveying a positive view of American history,” the letter said. This summer, the administration ordered the National Park Service to put up signs that would attempt to discredit the National Museum of American History. Throughout the pressure campaign, many have praised Bunch for sheltering the museums from the worst of it. Shanita Brackett, the current acting director of the National Museum of African American History and Culture, told our colleague Clint Smith earlier this year that Bunch has set an example in tough times. “What he’s taught us over the last 14 months is not to be afraid, not to run,” Brackett said. Bunch had become more direct in standing up for the institution in recent months, telling CNN’s Christiane Amanpour this summer that he believes “it’s our job to tell an accurate, complex, and truthful history,” and that “it scares me when people aren’t brave enough to face their history.” Still, Bunch’s legacy goes beyond holding the line between a beloved American institution and an overbearing president. Bunch first joined the institution in 1978, starting at the Air and Space Museum, and he later took on a number of roles at the American History Museum from 1989 to 2000, including associate director of curatorial affairs. After serving as the president of the Chicago Historical Society, he returned in 2005 to the Smithsonian to build the National Museum of African American History and Culture, which opened in 2016. Under his watch, two more museums were authorized by Congress: the American Women’s History Museum and the National Museum of the American Latino. Both have faced political roadblocks; the regents recently voted to open the Latino museum in a prominent building on the National Mall that the Smithsonian already controls in order for it to move forward without having to rely on Congress or the Trump administration to grant it a new site. At the town hall today, Bunch cited the plans for the new museums, the return of pandas to the National Zoo, and the celebrations for the country’s 250th birthday as among his proudest accomplishments. A show he co-curated, likely his final for the Smithsonian, was mounted at the Smithsonian Castle this summer, offering a subtle rejoinder to those who think history is a finished story, meant only for finding glory. The framing of this show for the country’s birthday, according to its introductory text, was Thomas Jefferson’s urging that the newly formed United States be a nation “in pursuit.”

The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye
2026-09-08

The Novel That Looks Death in the Eye

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The human brain has a method for dealing with the intrinsic fear of death. Terror-management theory, which builds on the work of the cultural anthropologist Ernest Becker, explains that the psyche handles this fear in a variety of ways. Many people either escape reality or adopt a belief system that promises immortality: so on the one hand, sex, drugs, or entertainment; on the other, religion, fame, or careerism. Or we might immerse ourselves in family, or culture—something that imbues life with meaning. Otherwise, the theory goes, we would be cognitively immobilized by impending doom. In her gut-twisting third novel, Switzy, Emma Cline does not abide by the tenets of terror management. Instead, she pulls death out from behind the curtain and forces her readers to look it in the eye for more than 300 pages. Switzy follows the final few days in the life of David Hastings, a former high-flying executive who’s been forced into retirement, and obsolescence, by an Alzheimer’s diagnosis. He has taken advantage of Switzerland’s right-to-die laws, paying the fees (a paltry sum for a wealthy man) and filling out the required paperwork. Now he’ll swallow some barbiturates and die in an impersonal room just outside Zurich, far from his family and his home in America. Cline’s approach is merciless—not toward David, for whom she shows tenderness and grace, but toward the reader, who will be nearly flattened by this novel. Or so I sincerely hope, because death ought to flatten us once in a while. Switzy begins mid-flight, as David hurtles across the Atlantic in a friend’s private jet. Accompanied by Cody—a former corporate underling who now functions as David’s caretaker, body man, and secretary—he lands first in London. There, he has dinner with his only child, an emotionally distant daughter who doesn’t know about her father’s decline, let alone his upcoming euthanasia. David tours a museum, visits his grandson, reboards the plane for his final flight, to Zurich; he dazedly lets Cody steer him around as a parent would guide a toddler. The younger man negotiates the intricacies of the world on David’s behalf, buffering him from the demands of day-to-day life—buying a museum ticket, sending a text message, procuring beverages. David needs the help: He is at times absent in his own mind. As rendered by Cline in a wandering, dreamlike style, this becomes a startling and eerie phenomenon—like the moment you realize you’ve been driving for miles but don’t recall anything about the journey. Far from experiencing the blissful ignorance that some people might project onto dementia patients, David is all too aware that his brain has locked away basic memories and social customs. He writes his grandson’s name, Rainer, in a notebook so he can recall it; a clock “in big kindergarten letters” informs him of the date and time each morning; he stares too long at someone in the museum and provokes his annoyance. And he sees each gap, knows that it signals another loss. “Work,” he thinks as he chews a croissant, “everything seemed like work. Even these good things—even this buttered mouthful.” Read: A different way to think about medicine’s most stubborn enigma The first half of Switzy crawls by, but it feels achingly necessary. This is time the way David experiences it, slowed down and sometimes skipped over: Alzheimer’s novels are rare, and those told from the patient’s point of view are even rarer. Cline, always a graceful stylist, employs a kind of abbreviated modernism to leap among David’s thoughts. They skitter backwards to memories of his failed marriage and his time at a boys’ boarding school. They run in circles, around catchy sayings—such as a mnemonic about Henry VIII’s abbreviated marriages, “Divorced, beheaded, died; divorced, beheaded, survived”—that Alzheimer’s hasn’t stolen from him. And they jump ahead to the moment and manner of his death, which inches closer with every turned page. Cline’s last novel, The Guest, also followed a character who hastily approaches a reckoning. Alex, a poor and wily 22-year-old sex worker who has just been kicked out of the home of her most recent catch, Simon, has to make it through five days in the Hamptons, until she can plead to be let back into the high-end milieu of Simon’s Labor Day party. Dead-eyed and mutable, Alex, like David, is trying to put on just enough of a show to pass muster with the people around her. For her, a different kind of death awaits—complete impoverishment, and removal from a perch where she may or may not belong. Switzy raises the stakes on this kind of quest. As the novel’s second half picks up steam, so does the cadence of David’s confrontations with the fact of his looming extinction. When the plane lands in Zurich, an official comes on to inspect their passports. “David smiled to himself. It was obvious, wasn’t it? This man was Death. Why else would he know to come right onboard?” Soon after, he sees Death again as a waiter, a busboy, two restaurant patrons. On his last night, he wakes repeatedly in his hotel, checking the sky’s brightness as a sign of how much time he has left. An Omega watch advertisement catches his eye, and though he doesn’t say it, the meaning is clear: Omega is the last letter in the Greek alphabet, the universal symbol of finality. As long as David cannot stop returning to his ticking clock, neither can you. This kind of sustained attention to the one topic many people spend their lives avoiding is practically subversive—a thrill, really. And it’s possibly a public service too. Studies have repeatedly shown that people who think about death on a regular basis are happier, because contemplating mortality reinforces the idea, as Emily Dickinson put it, “That it will never come again/ Is what makes life so sweet.” I daresay most readers won’t feel much happiness reading Switzy—though it does have glimmers of humor—but they might experience moments of sublime clarity. Death functions here in a way that isn’t common in fiction. Its presence doesn’t make this novel a tearjerker—it’s not, for example, Maggie O’Farrell’s Hamnet, which beautifully laments the loss of a child. It offers no spectacle akin to Anne Boleyn’s trembling march to the scaffold at the end of Hilary Mantel’s singular historical novel, Bring Up the Bodies. Unlike many 19th-century novels, Switzy doesn’t use death to solve a social problem, à la Anna Karenina (romantic devastation) and Madame Bovary (financial ruin). Nor is death here a handy plot device, as it is in so many works across the past century. Switzy is not about the living who are left behind. Cline’s approach to death marks different territory: It’s about raw, undeniable truth. Read: Charles Manson, the girls, and the banality of desire Dying is a transformative event: People pass from the only reality they’ve ever been able to recognize into something nebulous and ungraspable (even for the most fervent religious believers). And this might sound obvious, but you, reader, will die. So will I. Much of humanity has raised its defenses high against death, perhaps unintentionally, as Ernest Becker proposes. So how will it feel to see a man marching straight toward it—not unflinchingly, or tremulously, but with a perfectly ordinary mixture of confusion and acceptance? In Switzy’s final pages, David becomes a man who cannot help but be alive, even in his last moments. He doesn’t want to eat breakfast, but he feels it’s “better to have something in the stomach.” He feels irritated that he is given a blue pen to sign documents—“blue ink was for grocery lists.” He fumbles over his last words. Cline writes, “His was both the realest life in the room and the most unreal.” The line between everything and nothing is as thin as a breath of air. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

The Myth of Perfect Knowledge
2026-09-08

The Myth of Perfect Knowledge

The Atlantic | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
The modern world owes a lot to the Enlightenment, the 18th-century intellectual movement that emphasized the power of reason. But the Enlightenment also created a myth: that we can get to know the objective world completely, with total clarity, as if we were looking at it from the outside. This myth is challenged by the Enlightenment’s greatest legacy—modern science. Reality is more complex than we expected. Physics—my trade, and perhaps the most objective among the sciences—questions the possibility of a full description of how things objectively are. An electron can be in a “quantum superposition,” so that to an observer, it doesn’t occupy just one location—and so can you, my reader. This does not mean that you could perceive being dislocated between two positions; as perceived by yourself, you are always in just one place. But an object’s properties may differ depending on who, or what, is looking at them—or may not even be defined at all. Properties are not simple features of things: They are relations between one thing and another. What we call “the world” is therefore only how some parts of reality manifest themselves to other parts—us, for instance. Humanity has always been aware that our understanding is partial and biased. But the fact that an objective and knowable reality is incompatible with the grammar of physics is astonishing. The Enlightenment could consider the mind as being outside the world, in an abstract realm of reason where knowledge resides. But human minds are not outside reality, watching it from its exterior. We, like everything else, are part of the very nature we study. Our knowledge is only one example of the many correlations that intertwine different parts of nature. In the early 20th century, Niels Bohr, a founder of quantum theory, realized that quantum phenomena indicate that we cannot discard our own physical nature when we observe the world. The ideal of grasping objective reality by making the observer irrelevant is incompatible with physics. We are quantum processes interacting with other quantum processes. Our knowledge is a rich but incomplete set of correlations between one piece of the world–ourselves–and the rest of it. Adam Frank: The truth physics can no longer ignore To see this fact from another point of view, consider saying “It’s raining and Ann does not know it.” This makes sense: You know that it’s raining and that Ann doesn’t. But if you replace “Ann” with a first-person pronoun, the result is odd: “It’s raining and I do not know it.” How can you declare that it’s raining if you do not know it? This observation points to the fact that every proposition can only express the knowledge of the speaker or writer. No proposition is asserted from outside reality, because there is nothing outside reality. To say “It is raining” is really to say: “I, as part of reality, know that it’s raining.” Objective statements independent of any knower are fictions. Purely objective reality is a useful myth, but only a myth. Anything said about the state of the world comes from someone’s singular perspective. This means that all of our knowledge is intrinsically uncertain. All statements have unavoidable margins of uncertainty. Indeed, we can be wrong about anything, even the basic facts of our own lives. My father grew up sure that his first name was Franco until he asked for a copy of his birth certificate and found out that my grandfather, without telling my grandma, had registered him as Giulio. He had been wrong about his own name. Humanity has been collectively wrong over and over again. We thought the sun moved around the earth, then learned that it is the earth that orbits the sun. Great scientists are even more likely to be wrong than others, because they have the courage to try—Einstein’s published work includes many mistakes. But in science, as in daily life, we do not need certainty; being reasonably sure is good enough. If I have an airplane to catch, I will go to the airport even though I’m not totally sure that the flight will take off; if I waited for certainty, I’d never leave home. Modern science offers an enormous body of knowledge that is effective and, in many domains, far better than all the alternatives, but it gives us no absolute certainty, no ultimate story, no Truth with a capital T. Within science, there are different ways of describing the same things. A glass of water can be described in terms of physics (its mass, velocity), thermodynamics (its temperature), chemistry (there is more than just H2O in any glass of water), biology (bacteria swim in it), particle physics (its electrons, protons, and neutrons), psychology (“Why did I fill the glass?”), economics (its price), and more. Each account is valid in its own terms; each reflects a way in which the same object interacts with different parts of reality, from atoms to the global economy. But none exhausts an “ultimate truth” of the glass. The same is true for human beings. I can think of you, my reader, as a body, as a mind, or as a soul. These aren’t different entities: They are the same entity, described in different languages, using different conceptual schemes, focusing on different aspects of the same you and different ways in which you interact with other parts of reality. There is no sharp separation or metaphysical gap between body and soul; matter and spirit; the physical world and the world of laws, ideas, and norms. In this sense, all things are equal: They are all aspects of the same natural world, considered from different perspectives, none of which is the ultimate one. Everything can be considered fundamental from one perspective or another. The expression “the equality of all things” comes from the title of the second chapter of The Book of Zhuangzi, one of the greatest books of humankind—an ancient classic of Chinese philosophy that articulates a similar intriguing perspectival and anti-foundational naturalism. This is the view to which I think modern science leads us. Many people resist it, some out of fear of losing solid reference points: objectivity, certainties, absolute morality, an immortal soul. But scientific knowledge does not become less valuable if you remain open to doubt. Morality does not lose power if you recognize that it is rooted in our biology, culture, and history. Our values do not weaken because they are rooted in what we are, not in something outside us. And life is even more beautiful and precious without dreams of immortality. We are not—as French existentialism described us—thrown into alien nature. In nature, we are home. In fact, it seems to me that recognizing the perspectival aspect of knowledge, values, and beliefs, and of reality itself, could help us become a bit less arrogant, a bit humbler and wiser. Conflicts between individuals or countries don’t stem only from greed and thirst for power. They are also fueled by the belief that we are objectively right and the enemy is objectively wrong, which ignores the plurality of perspectives. We have our values that guide us, but understanding the perspective of others is the first step toward wisdom—and toward peace. In our era of nuclear armaments, ignoring that others have different perspectives, and failing to respect them, could drag the world into a catastrophe.

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend
2026-09-08

Jared Kushner’s Incompetence Was on Full Display This Weekend

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-08

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It is a mystery why President Trump continues to rely on the hapless team of Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff—his son-in-law and fellow real-estate tycoon, respectively—to hammer out treaties to end the most destructive wars of our time: Russia vs. Ukraine and Israel vs. the Palestinians. Yes, Trump doesn’t trust the professional diplomatic corps or experts of any kind. But, as a self-proclaimed businessman, he must notice when aides don’t get the job done, and the Kushner-Witkoff duo have not lured the combatants one step closer to peace; in fact, they’ve arguably intensified the war in Ukraine. Their utter incompetence was on full display over the weekend, as they met with Vladimir Putin in Moscow, then—in their first trip to the Ukrainian capital—Volodymyr Zelensky in Kyiv. They carried what Trump touted ahead of time as new peace proposals, and concluded both sets of talks with boilerplate adjectives (“productive,” “meaningful,” “positive,” “businesslike”). But in fact, they came away with zilch—and, once again, proved themselves far short of the task. This should come as no surprise. Neither has any diplomatic experience; neither knows anything about Russia or Ukraine or military strategy; nor do they travel with any specialists who might advise them along the way. Witkoff kicked off the tour with an act of full-bore genuflection toward Putin, greeting the Kremlin dictator as follows: First, Mr. President, thank you so much for having us. We were sitting outside just now, me, Jared, and Kirill [Dmitriev], and then Yuri [Ushakov] joined us, and we were all talking about when we retire, we’ll have all these incredible stories and incredible memories, and yours will be right at the top of it all. In other words, Mr. President, he might have added, we come to praise you, not pressure you into taking any actions that you don’t feel like taking. Later in the day, the aforementioned Ushakov, Putin’s senior diplomat, released a summary of the Kremlin team’s position at the meeting: The Russian army has had battlefield successes, meaning it might still win the war; Ukrainian troops must withdraw from the eastern Donbas regions; the war’s “root cause” is Zelensky’s belief that Ukraine is an independent nation; and peace will bring many business opportunities between Russia and the United States. In other words, the usual propaganda. (The U.S. envoys did not release an official “readout” of the meeting, perhaps because they are not officials. That’s right: Kushner and Witkoff hold no government posts, have not been confirmed by the Senate, and are unaccountable to anyone but Trump.) The duo then flew to Poland and from there proceeded to Kyiv by train. (Zelensky had promised not to bomb Moscow while the two were in the Kremlin, but Putin offered no such pledge in return, so the U.S. security team felt Kyiv’s airport was unsafe. In any case, Putin resumed bombarding Ukraine’s capital city shortly after the two departed. Such a good friend.) After this meeting, Zelensky insisted that the three hold a press conference, mainly with Ukrainian reporters. It did not go well. Asked if he felt “ashamed” to meet at the same table with an international war criminal like Putin, Witkoff replied: We’ve always been treated respectfully by President Putin and his team. Relationships are critical in terms of solving conflicts like this. My job, and Jared’s job, is to bring the parties together. All three sentences reflect, and reaffirm, Witkoff’s utter naivete. They have not been treated respectfully; hence the refusal to issue a hands-off order on Kyiv’s airport while the two were in town. Relationships are not critical in solving conflicts; managing interests, either through diplomacy or force, is key. (Trump, too, doesn’t understand this.) Finally, if Witkoff thinks his job is simply to bring the parties together, he doesn’t understand his job. Kushner repeated much of Witkoff’s nonsense, saying that the point of negotiations is to produce a “win-win,” including some sort of favor that fulfills Russia’s “aspirations.” Since Russia’s chief aspirations—as Putin himself has declared—are to obliterate Ukraine as an independent nation and to restore much of the old Russian Empire, this isn’t likely to be the basis for an equitable peace. Along the same lines, Kushner expressed hope that the U.S., Russia, and Ukraine could resume the trilateral talks that began shortly before Trump got sidetracked by the war on Iran (though Kushner didn’t express the chronology that way). The few talks that took place were, as he recalled, such jolly affairs. “Everyone was speaking in Russian to each other. We were discussing these issues in a very extra-friendly manner, all in Russian.” If he’d been escorted by a specialist, Kushner might have been informed that speaking Russian doesn’t make Ukrainians feel merry and welcome, especially with Russian officials who want to be their overlords. (Many Ukrainians these days regard the Russian language, including classic Russian literature, as inherently imperialist.) Another Kushner faux pas, this one perhaps reflecting his ignorance of European history: “There is no such thing in the world as forever enemies or forever allies.” He said this in the context of urging Ukrainians to imagine that they might once again be friendly with Russians. However, the analogy that some observers thought he was invoking—the U.S. alliance with Germany after World War II—was not at all fitting, as it developed only after the U.S. and other allies defeated Nazi Germany, deposed its government, tried many of its leaders for war crimes, and imposed a new constitution. Even then, it wasn’t until 10 years after the war ended that the new Federal Republic of Germany was trusted enough for the allies to invite it to join NATO. Clearly, Kushner wasn’t proposing that Putin be killed or that his government be transformed—though many in the audience might have hoped that he would. It has never been entirely clear how Trump would like this war to end—or if he thinks it should end, or whether he cares one way or the other. Until he decides, keeping Kushner and Witkoff on the case is a waste of jet fuel and time. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
